**James 1:13–15 — temptation and the origin of sin.** James expressly denies that God is the source of evil temptation. He describes each person as tempted when drawn away and enticed by “his own lust.” Lust conceives and brings forth sin; completed sin brings forth death. This is a moral progression in which human desire and conduct are expressly involved. It must not be converted into a claim that God authors sin.

**James 1:16 — warning against error.** “Do not err” links the warning to the preceding explanation and introduces the positive account of God's character and gifts. The reader must not attribute to God what James has just denied of Him.

**James 1:17 — the Father of lights.** Every good gift and every perfect gift comes from above, from the Father of lights. Unlike created lights that vary or cast changing shadows, God has no variableness or shadow of turning. The description grounds the contrast in God's consistent goodness. The verse does not describe God as the source of temptation; it identifies Him as the source of good and perfect gifts.

**James 1:18 — God's will and the Word of Truth.** “Of his own will” precedes and qualifies “begat he us.” God is the acting subject. The verb is active; those addressed are the recipients of the begetting. “With the word of truth” identifies the Word in connection with this act. The stated purpose or result is that the believers should be “a kind of firstfruits of his creatures.”

**James 1:19–27 — the response that follows.** The following context commands hearing, speaking, anger, receiving with meekness the engrafted word, and being doers of the word rather than hearers only. James therefore does not erase human response or obedience. He distinguishes the divine begetting in verse 18 from the conduct required of those who hear the Word in the following verses. The command to receive the Word in meekness (v. 21) should be read in its own place; it does not change the grammatical subject of “begat he us” in verse 18.

### D. Canonical comparisons and counter-texts

**John 1:12–13.** Those who receive Christ and believe on His name are given the right to become the sons of God (v. 12); verse 13 describes their birth as not of blood, the will of the flesh, or the will of man, but of God. This is a close comparison because both passages identify God as the source of birth. John 1 also expressly mentions believing, whereas James 1:18 does not. Their distinct statements must be preserved.

**Ephesians 2:1–10.** Paul describes those dead in trespasses as made alive together with Christ by God, saved by grace through faith, not from themselves or works, and created in Christ for good works prepared beforehand. This complements James's attribution of begetting to God's own will. Ephesians explicitly names faith; James 1:18 names the Word of Truth. Neither passage should be made to say the other's exact wording.

**1 Peter 1:3, 23–25.** Peter blesses God for having begotten believers again according to His abundant mercy through the resurrection of Jesus Christ (v. 3), and later says they are born again not of corruptible seed but incorruptible, by the Word of God which lives and abides forever (vv. 23–25). This is a strong comparison for divine mercy, new birth, and the enduring Word. The exact relation between the Word and the divine act should be examined in Peter's own construction.

**1 John 5:1.** “Whosoever believeth that Jesus is the Christ is born of God.” The perfect-form birth language and present believing require careful examination. This text is directly relevant to the relation between believing and having been born of God. It is retained for its own dedicated entry; it must not be settled by importing a chronology into James 1:18.

**Philippians 1:29.** Paul says that it is given to believers on behalf of Christ not only to believe on Him but also to suffer for His sake. This is relevant to the divine granting of believing, but the verse's context and the scope of “it is given” require separate examination. It does not add the word “faith” to James 1:18.

**John 3:1–8.** Jesus teaches that birth of the Spirit is necessary to see and enter the kingdom and that what is born of the Spirit is spirit. This provides a distinct teaching about the Spirit as the source of spiritual birth. James 1:18 identifies God's will and the Word of Truth; the passages may illuminate one another without being collapsed.

### E. Original-language findings

- **βουληθεὶς (*boulētheis*)**, “of his own will” (v. 18): aorist passive-form participle, nominative masculine singular, from βούλομαι (*boulomai*), “will, intend, wish, purpose.” The verb is deponent in this usage; the participle has active meaning, “having willed” or “because/when He willed,” according to how the participial relation is expressed in translation. It agrees with the implied subject, God. The phrase identifies God's willing; it does not name a human will as the source of the begetting.
- **ἀπεκύησεν (*apekyēsen*)**, “begat” (v. 18): aorist active indicative, third person singular, from ἀποκυέω (*apokyeō*), “bring forth” or “give birth.” God is the grammatical subject. The verb is used in James 1:15 of lust bringing forth sin and in 1:18 of God bringing forth “us.” The shared birth imagery creates a literary contrast between the progression from lust to sin/death and God's begetting of believers. It does not make the two sources morally or causally equivalent.
- **ἡμᾶς (*hēmas*)**, “us” (v. 18): accusative first-person plural pronoun, the object of the active verb. Those addressed are recipients of God's begetting.
- **ἐν λόγῳ ἀληθείας (*en logō alētheias*)**, “with the word of truth” (v. 18): ἐν (*en*) with the dative may express sphere, means, or instrument, depending on context. λόγῳ (*logō*) is dative singular of λόγος (*logos*), “word/message”; ἀληθείας (*alētheias*) is genitive singular of ἀλήθεια (*alētheia*), “truth.” The Word of Truth is expressly connected with the begetting. The precise nuance of ἐν should not be overstated; “with” is the KJV rendering, while “by/through” is a possible instrumental interpretation.
- **εἰς τὸ εἶναι (*eis to einai*)**, “that we should be” (v. 18): εἰς with the articular infinitive expresses purpose or intended result. The phrase introduces the stated end of the begetting.
- **ἀπαρχήν (*aparchēn*)**, “firstfruits” (v. 18): accusative singular of ἀπαρχή (*aparchē*), first portion or firstfruits, often carrying the idea of an initial portion set apart and representative of a larger whole. “A kind of” reflects the wording of the KJV and should not be dropped when discussing the phrase.
- **κτισμάτων (*ktismatōn*)**, “creatures” (v. 18): genitive plural of κτίσμα (*ktisma*), that which is created. The phrase places the recipients in relation to God's created order. The exact scope of “his creatures” and the force of “firstfruits” should be read in the wider argument rather than assigned an unsupported timetable.
- **πατρὸς τῶν φώτων (*patros tōn phōtōn*)**, “Father of lights” (v. 17): the genitive plural “of lights” is associated with the Father. The image is commonly understood in relation to the heavenly lights, but the central point in this context is God's unchanging goodness in contrast with variation and shadow. Do not make the metaphor carry claims that James does not state.
- **παραλλαγή (*parallagē*)** and **τροπῆς ἀποσκίασμα (*tropēs aposkiasma*)** (v. 17): terms for variation/change and a shadow associated with turning or shifting. James contrasts the changing appearance of lights with God's constancy. The verse is a character statement about God, not a technical account of astronomy.

### F. What the passage establishes

| Category | Finding | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| God as source of good gifts | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | Every good and perfect gift comes from above, from the Father (v. 17). |
| God's unchanging character | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | No variableness or shadow of turning (v. 17). |
| God's will in new birth | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “Of his own will begat he us” (v. 18). |
| God as acting subject of begetting | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | God is the implied subject of the active verb ἀπεκύησεν. |
| Human beings as recipients of the begetting | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “Us” is the object of the verb (v. 18). |
| Word of Truth connected with begetting | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | The phrase ἐν λόγῳ ἀληθείας occurs in verse 18. |
| Exact instrumental/sphere nuance of ἐν | GRAMMATICAL QUESTION | The KJV says “with”; an instrumental rendering is possible, but the precise nuance should remain qualified. |
| Purpose/result of the begetting | EXPLICITLY STATED | The believers are to be a kind of firstfruits of God's creatures (v. 18). |
| Human response and obedience | EXPLICIT IN THE FOLLOWING CONTEXT | James commands receiving the Word and being doers (vv. 19–27). |
| Faith as the instrument in this particular verse | NOT STATED | James 1:18 does not use the word πίστις (faith). |
| Complete chronology of regeneration and faith | NOT SETTLED | The verse identifies divine will and begetting but does not describe a full sequence involving faith. |
| Autonomous human will as the source of new birth | NOT ESTABLISHED; CONTRARY TO THE STATED SOURCE | The verse identifies God's own will as the ground of the begetting. |

### G. Triple Constraint disposition

- **Biblical compliance — PASS, within scope.** The reading preserves James 1:13–18 as a connected argument: God is not the source of evil temptation; human lust is involved in the progression to sin; good gifts come from the Father; and God begets believers of His own will through/in connection with the Word of Truth. The commands and human response in verses 19–27 remain real.
- **Scientific coherence — PASS, within scope.** The grammatical observations distinguish the participle βουληθείς from the active finite verb ἀπεκύησεν, identify the grammatical subject and object, and preserve the range of ἐν. No chronology is inferred from tense alone.
- **GUA-W consistency — PASS, within scope.** God is identified as the source and acting subject of the begetting. The Word of Truth is connected with His action. The recipients do not author their own birth. No new architecture is proposed.

### H. Entry finding and limits

James 1:13–18 presents a clear contrast. The progression toward sin and death is associated with a person's own lust and the actions that follow. God, by contrast, is the unchanging Father of lights and the source of every good and perfect gift. James then states that God, “of his own will,” begat believers with the Word of Truth, with the intended result that they should be a kind of firstfruits of His creatures.

The text explicitly identifies God's will as the ground of the begetting and God as its acting subject. It connects the Word of Truth with that begetting. It does not mention faith in verse 18, define every aspect of human willing, or give a complete chronological account of regeneration and faith. The commands to hear, receive, and do the Word in the following verses demonstrate that divine begetting does not erase human response and obedience.

**Finding:** James 1:18 does not identify autonomous human will as the originating cause of new birth. It identifies God's own will as the stated source of the begetting and the Word of Truth as connected with that act. The precise instrumental nuance of ἐν and the full canonical chronology of faith and regeneration remain open.

**Status:** PASS for God's own will as the stated ground of begetting; PASS for God's agency and the connection with the Word of Truth; PARTIAL for the exact nuance of “with/by” and the scope of “firstfruits”; UNRESOLVED for the full chronology of new birth and faith and for an exhaustive definition of the human will.

### I. Related GUA-W recognition and cross-references

This passage witnesses a distinction between the source of an act and the means or word associated with it. God's own will is named; God performs the begetting; the Word of Truth is connected with the act; the recipients are brought forth and designated as firstfruits. This is a textual recognition only, not a proposal to create or modify GUA-W architecture.

Cross-references for continued examination:
- John 1:12–13 — receiving/believing and birth of God;
- John 3:1–8 — birth of the Spirit;
- Ephesians 2:1–10 — quickening, grace, faith, and workmanship;
- 1 Peter 1:3, 23–25 — begetting again and the living Word;
- 1 John 5:1 — believing and having been born of God;
- Philippians 1:29 — believing granted.

### J. Research queue

1. HW-010 — Philippians 2:12–13: examine human working, God's working in believers, and the relationship between willing and doing.
2. Retain 1 John 5:1 as a priority passage for the grammatical and contextual relation between believing and having been born of God.
3. Examine 1 Peter 1:3, 23–25 for the relationship between God's mercy, resurrection, new birth, and the Word.
4. Keep U-006 (definition and components of human will) open. James 1 distinguishes human lust in temptation from God's will in begetting, but it does not provide an exhaustive definition of the human will.

---


### Canonical completion review — 2026-11-18

**Original limitation reviewed:** ** The relation of God's begetting by His own will and the Word to the believer's faith and new birth needs comparison.

**Canonical passages examined:** **John 1:12–13 (HW-001); John 3:3–8 (HW-005); 1 Peter 1:23; 1 John 5:1 (HW-014); Ephesians 2:1–10 (HW-008); Titus 3:5 (HW-019).**

**Combined biblical finding:** James explicitly says God begat us of His own will with the Word of truth. John 1 and 3 identify God/Spirit as the source of birth. First Peter 1:23 connects being born again with the incorruptible Word. First John 5:1 identifies the believer as born of God. Titus names regeneration and renewal of the Holy Ghost.

**Revised disposition:** **ANSWERED as to the stated source of begetting: God's own will. NARROWED as to the relation between new birth, Word, Spirit, and believing.** The exact chronology and full mechanism of the first act of faith are not exhaustively set out by these texts.

**Synchronization instruction:** This disposition supplements the original passage analysis and governs the classification of the limitation above. Where an earlier table says only “not established by this passage alone” or “unresolved,” read that statement as a limit of the assigned passage, not as a conclusion about the whole canon. The cited passages must remain in their own contexts. Any remaining question is limited to the precise boundary stated here; no duplicate HW investigation is created.

## 16. Entry — HW-010: Philippians 2:12–13 (2026-11-18)

### A. Exact KJV passage

> **Philippians 2:12–13**
>
> 12 Wherefore, my beloved, as ye have always obeyed, not as in my presence only, but now much more in my absence, work out your own salvation with fear and trembling.
>
> 13 For it is God which worketh in you both to will and to do of his good pleasure.

The immediate literary context begins earlier. Philippians 2:1–4 addresses unity, humility, and regard for others. Verses 5–11 present Christ's humility, obedience unto death, and exaltation by God. “Wherefore” in verse 12 connects the believers' commanded conduct with that preceding Christological presentation. Verses 14–18 describe the conduct and witness expected of the community.

### B. Argument commonly made

Philippians 2:12 is sometimes presented as proof that salvation is ultimately a human work: believers are commanded to “work out” their salvation. This reading overlooks verse 13, which gives the reason for the command and identifies God as working in believers both to will and to do. Paul is not commanding believers to purchase salvation or originate it independently.

The opposite error is to use verse 13 to deny real human willing, obedience, or action. Verse 12 expressly addresses the believers as responsible agents and commands them to work out their salvation. The following verses command concrete conduct. Paul does not erase human activity when he identifies God's operation.

The passage holds both statements together: believers are commanded to work out their salvation, and God works in them both to will and to do. The text does not present these statements as competitors. Nor does it provide a technical account of every causal or chronological relation between divine operation and human willing.

### C. Immediate context and argument

**Philippians 2:1–4 — unity and humility.** Paul appeals to shared encouragement in Christ, comfort of love, fellowship of the Spirit, and compassion. He commands the church to be likeminded, to avoid strife and vainglory, and in humility to esteem others. Each is to look not only on his own things but also on the things of others. The plural address and community-focused commands frame the passage.

**Philippians 2:5–11 — Christ's example and exaltation.** Paul directs the believers to the mind that was also in Christ Jesus. Christ, being in the form of God, took the form of a servant, humbled Himself, and became obedient unto death, even the death of the cross. God highly exalted Him. The “Wherefore” of verse 12 must be read in relation to this account of Christ's humility, obedience, and exaltation. The text is not an isolated philosophical treatise on free will.

**Philippians 2:12 — the believers' commanded working.** Paul acknowledges their obedience, both in his presence and more abundantly in his absence, and commands them to “work out” their own salvation with fear and trembling. The imperative is plural, addressed to the community. “Work out” must not be changed into “work for” as though Paul were describing the purchase or earning of salvation. The command concerns the lived expression and outworking of salvation in the believers' conduct. Its precise relation to the whole scope of salvation, including its final consummation, should be read in the wider letter.

**Philippians 2:13 — God's operation.** “For” gives the reason or ground for the preceding command. God is the stated subject of “worketh.” His operation is “in you” and is expressly related to both willing and doing. The phrase “of his good pleasure” locates the purpose or good pleasure with God. The verse does not say merely that God assists the execution of an independently originated human intention; willing itself is included in what God works in believers.

**Philippians 2:14–18 — visible conduct.** Paul commands them to do all things without murmurings and disputings, to be blameless and harmless, to shine as lights in the world, and to hold forth the word of life. Their conduct is visible and consequential. Paul speaks of his labor, service, rejoicing, and their rejoicing with him. God's operation in verse 13 does not cancel the commands and actions in this following context.

**Philippians 1:27–30 — conduct worthy of the gospel.** The preceding chapter has already called the church to stand fast in one spirit, with one mind striving together for the faith of the gospel. This further confirms the corporate and practical setting of the command in 2:12.

### D. Original-language findings

- **ὥστε (*hōste*)**, “wherefore/so then” (v. 12): a connective that draws an inference or consequence from the preceding argument. The KJV's “Wherefore” correctly signals that verse 12 is not detached from the Christological material in verses 5–11.
- **ὑπηκούσατε (*hypēkousate*)**, “ye have obeyed” (v. 12): aorist active indicative, second person plural, from ὑπακούω (*hypakouō*), “obey, heed.” Paul recognizes actual obedience by the Philippians. This is not a hypothetical or merely apparent response.
- **κατεργάζεσθε (*katergazesthe*)**, “work out” (v. 12): present middle imperative, second person plural, from κατεργάζομαι (*katergazomai*), “work out, bring about, accomplish, carry through.” It is a command directed to the believers as a group. The middle form should not be made to mean that they are the independent originating cause of salvation. “Work out” is not equivalent to “earn” or “purchase.”
- **τὴν ἑαυτῶν σωτηρίαν (*tēn heautōn sōtērian*)**, “your own salvation” (v. 12): σωτηρία (*sōtēria*) means salvation, deliverance, or rescue; ἑαυτῶν is a plural reflexive genitive, “of yourselves/your own.” The plural wording and surrounding church instructions support a corporate dimension, without excluding the participation of each believer. The verse does not by itself settle every aspect of the temporal scope of salvation.
- **μετὰ φόβου καὶ τρόμου (*meta phobou kai tromou*)**, “with fear and trembling” (v. 12): a phrase expressing seriousness, reverence, and sober concern in the presence of God. It should not be flattened into mere panic, nor should it be made to mean that believers must earn acceptance through anxiety.
- **θεὸς γάρ (*theos gar*)**, “for it is God” (v. 13): γάρ (*gar*) supplies the reason or explanation for the preceding command. θεός (*theos*) is the subject of the following participial construction. The explanatory connection is essential: the command to work out is grounded in God's operation.
- **ὁ ἐνεργῶν (*ho energōn*)**, “which worketh” (v. 13): present active participle, nominative masculine singular, from ἐνεργέω (*energeō*), “work, be active, operate.” It identifies God as the one actively operating. The present form portrays the operation as in progress or characteristic; tense alone does not supply a complete chronology.
- **ἐν ὑμῖν (*en hymin*)**, “in you” (v. 13): ἐν (*en*) with the dative plural pronoun. It can be rendered “in you” and, in this plural church context, may carry a corporate dimension (“among you”) as well. The context includes both the believers' shared life and the operation within them; neither dimension should be dogmatically excluded without further argument.
- **τὸ θέλειν (*to thelein*)**, “to will” (v. 13): an articular present active infinitive from θέλω (*thelō*), “will, wish, desire.” It is a substantival infinitive, naming willing as an activity or capacity involved in God's operation. The wording expressly includes willing; it does not merely describe divine assistance with the outward execution of a prior independent intention.
- **καὶ τὸ ἐνεργεῖν (*kai to energein*)**, “and to do” (v. 13): an articular present active infinitive from ἐνεργέω (*energeō*), “work, operate, accomplish.” It is coordinated with “to will.” Both willing and doing are included in what God works in believers. The shared lexical family between God's “working” (ὁ ἐνεργῶν) and the believers' “doing/working” (τὸ ἐνεργεῖν) should be noticed without confusing their grammatical roles.
- **ὑπὲρ τῆς εὐδοκίας (*hyper tēs eudokias*)**, “of his good pleasure” (v. 13): εὐδοκία (*eudokia*) denotes good pleasure, purpose, or favorable intention. The phrase locates the good pleasure with God in the context. The precise force of ὑπέρ (*hyper*) should be handled by the translation and context; the main point is that the willing and doing are related to God's good pleasure, not an independent human purpose.
- **Tense and sequence limitation:** The present forms in verse 13 portray God's operation and the willing/doing as ongoing or characteristic. They do not, by themselves, establish a measurable temporal sequence in which one event must precede the other. The aorist in “ye have obeyed” likewise does not provide a full chronology of salvation.

### E. Canonical comparisons and counter-texts

**Philippians 1:6.** Paul is confident that He who began a good work in the believers will perform it until the day of Jesus Christ. This provides a close letter-context comparison for divine initiation and continuation. It should be read with the letter's calls to faithful conduct, not as a denial of them.

**Philippians 1:27–30.** The church is commanded to stand fast and strive together for the faith of the gospel. Their conduct and perseverance are real, while suffering and believing are discussed in relation to what is granted to them. Verse 29 deserves a separate grammatical and contextual examination before drawing a complete conclusion about faith as a gift.

**Philippians 2:5–11.** Christ's humility and obedience provide the immediate foundation for the exhortation. The believers are called to a corresponding mind and conduct; Christ's unique identity and saving work must not be reduced to a mere moral example.

**Ephesians 2:8–10.** Salvation is by grace through faith, not from the recipients or from works; believers are God's workmanship, created in Christ for good works prepared beforehand. This parallels the distinction between salvation's source and the believer's resulting walk. Ephesians and Philippians should retain their own wording.

**James 1:18.** God begets believers of His own will with/in connection with the Word of Truth. This supports the recognition of God's initiative in new birth. Philippians 2:13 goes further in expressly naming God's operation in both willing and doing, but it addresses the lived obedience of believers rather than supplying a complete account of regeneration.

**Hebrews 13:20–21.** The prayer asks God to make believers perfect in every good work to do His will, “working in you that which is wellpleasing in his sight, through Jesus Christ.” This is a strong comparison for divine operation and human conduct directed toward God's will. It requires a separate full entry before being treated as independently adjudicated.

**1 Corinthians 15:10.** Paul says, “by the grace of God I am what I am,” and that God's grace was not bestowed in vain; he labored, yet “not I, but the grace of God which was with me.” This preserves both real labor and divine grace as the explanation of Paul's labor. It is a useful parallel but must be read in its own apostolic context.

**Galatians 2:20.** Paul describes his life as crucified with Christ and yet living, with Christ living in him; the life he now lives in the flesh is by faith of the Son of God. This further illustrates Paul's ability to speak of genuine human life and action while locating its governing source in Christ. It does not erase the human subject.

### F. What the passage establishes

| Category | Finding | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| Human obedience | EXPLICITLY ACKNOWLEDGED | Paul says the Philippians have obeyed (v. 12). |
| Human action | EXPLICITLY COMMANDED | They are commanded to work out their salvation (v. 12) and to act without murmurings (v. 14). |
| Human willing | EXPLICITLY INCLUDED | “To will” is named in verse 13. |
| God's operation | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | God is the subject of “worketh in you” (v. 13). |
| God's operation in willing | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | God's working includes “to will” (v. 13). |
| God's operation in doing | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | God's working includes “to do” (v. 13). |
| Salvation earned by human works | NOT ESTABLISHED | “Work out” is an imperative concerning lived salvation; it does not say “earn” or “purchase.” |
| Human responsibility | EXPLICITLY PRESENT | The commands and acknowledged obedience are real. |
| Corporate dimension | STRONGLY SUPPORTED BY CONTEXT | Plural pronouns and imperatives, unity, mutual regard, and communal witness frame the passage. |
| Individual participation | NOT EXCLUDED | Each member participates in the community's obedience and conduct. |
| Autonomous human will as the originating source of willing | NOT ESTABLISHED | God is expressly said to work in believers both to will and to do. |
| Complete chronology of divine operation and human willing | NOT SETTLED | The verb forms do not provide a complete temporal sequence. |
| Exhaustive definition of human will | NOT PROVIDED | The passage identifies willing as an activity in which God works, but does not define every faculty or aspect of the will. |

### G. Triple Constraint disposition

- **Biblical compliance — PASS, within scope.** The reading preserves the imperative to work out salvation, the believers' acknowledged obedience, and God's operation in both willing and doing. It reads the passage in the context of Christ's humility and exaltation and the church's unity and conduct.
- **Scientific coherence — PASS, within scope.** The Greek forms and syntactic relations are distinguished without treating tense or voice as a shortcut to a complete metaphysical chronology. The present participle and articular infinitives are identified; grammatical subject and commanded agents are not conflated.
- **GUA-W consistency — PASS, within scope.** The text identifies God as the one operating in believers, including in willing and doing. Human obedience remains a real derivative activity; it is not assigned the role of Original Cause. No architectural modification is proposed.

### H. Entry finding and limits

Philippians 2:12–13 does not permit either of two reductions. Verse 12 cannot be isolated to claim that believers originate or earn their salvation by independent human effort. Verse 13 cannot be used to deny that believers genuinely obey, will, act, and are commanded to conduct themselves faithfully.

Paul's argument holds the two together. The believers are commanded to work out their salvation with fear and trembling. The reason is that God works in them both to will and to do according to His good pleasure. God's operation is not restricted to outward assistance after an independently originated human decision; the text expressly includes willing. Yet the human command and activity remain real.

**Finding:** The passage explicitly identifies God as operating in believers in relation to both willing and doing. It does not identify autonomous human will as the originating source of the believer's willing. It also does not erase human willing, obedience, or responsibility. The passage does not provide a complete chronology of the relation between God's operation and each human act, nor an exhaustive philosophical definition of the will.

**Status:** PASS for God's operation in both willing and doing; PASS for genuine human obedience and action; FAIL for claiming that verse 12 teaches salvation is earned by human effort; FAIL for claiming that verse 13 makes human willing and action unreal; UNRESOLVED for a complete chronology and exhaustive definition of the human will.

### I. Related GUA-W recognition and cross-references

The passage witnesses the distinction between divine operation and human activity without separating them into rival causes. God works in believers; believers are commanded to work out their salvation. The text specifically includes willing and doing within God's operation. This is a textual recognition, not a proposal to create or modify GUA-W architecture.

Relevant established distinctions:
- God's operation versus human response;
- source versus commanded participation;
- willing versus doing;
- individual action within a corporate body;
- salvation as God's work versus good conduct as its lived expression.

No new GUA-W layer, Gear, or architectural component is proposed by this entry.

### J. Research queue

1. HW-011 — 1 Corinthians 2:10–16: examine the natural man, the things of the Spirit, spiritual discernment, and the mind of Christ.
2. HW-012 — Romans 8:5–11: examine flesh, Spirit, inability, indwelling, and life.
3. Retain Hebrews 13:20–21 and 1 Corinthians 15:10 as priority comparisons for divine operation and human doing.
4. Retain 1 John 5:1 as a priority passage for the grammatical and contextual relation between believing and having been born of God.
5. Keep U-006 (definition and components of human will) open. Philippians 2 establishes God's operation in willing and doing but does not provide an exhaustive definition of human will.

---


### Canonical completion review — 2026-11-18

**Original limitation reviewed:** ** The relation between believers' working/obedience and God's working in them “to will and to do” requires passages describing the Spirit's renewal and the believer's conduct.

**Canonical passages examined:** **Ezekiel 36:26–27; Jeremiah 31:31–34; Hebrews 13:20–21; Ephesians 2:8–10; Romans 8:12–14; 1 Corinthians 15:10; Philippians 1:6.**

**Combined biblical finding:** Ezekiel describes God giving a new heart and putting His Spirit within His people, causing them to walk in His statutes. Hebrews asks God to work in believers what is pleasing in His sight. Ephesians distinguishes grace as salvation's source from good works as the appointed walk. Romans 8 preserves Spirit-led action and responsibility. First Corinthians 15:10 joins Paul's labour with God's grace working in him.

**Revised disposition:** **ANSWERED as to the compatibility of divine operation and real human obedience: both are stated. NARROWED as to the complete order and mechanics of willing.** Philippians 2:13 explicitly attributes the operation in willing and doing to God; verse 12 still commands the believers' own working out. The text does not make either side unreal.

**Synchronization instruction:** This disposition supplements the original passage analysis and governs the classification of the limitation above. Where an earlier table says only “not established by this passage alone” or “unresolved,” read that statement as a limit of the assigned passage, not as a conclusion about the whole canon. The cited passages must remain in their own contexts. Any remaining question is limited to the precise boundary stated here; no duplicate HW investigation is created.

## 17. Entry — HW-011: 1 Corinthians 2:10–16 (2026-11-18)

### A. Exact KJV passage

> **1 Corinthians 2:10–16**
>
> 10 But God hath revealed them unto us by his Spirit: for the Spirit searcheth all things, yea, the deep things of God.
>
> 11 For what man knoweth the things of a man, save the spirit of man which is in him? even so the things of God knoweth no man, but the Spirit of God.
>
> 12 Now we have received, not the spirit of the world, but the spirit which is of God; that we might know the things that are freely given to us of God.
>
> 13 Which things also we speak, not in the words which man's wisdom teacheth, but which the Holy Ghost teacheth; comparing spiritual things with spiritual.
>
> 14 But the natural man receiveth not the things of the Spirit of God: for they are foolishness unto him: neither can he know them, because they are spiritually discerned.
>
> 15 But he that is spiritual judgeth all things, yet he himself is judged of no man.
>
> 16 For who hath known the mind of the Lord, that he may instruct him? But we have the mind of Christ.

The argument begins before verse 10. In 1 Corinthians 1:18–2:9 Paul contrasts the wisdom of God with the wisdom of the world, proclaims Christ crucified, and says that the rulers of this world did not know God's wisdom. The revelation in 2:10 is God's action through the Spirit. The passage then contrasts the Spirit's knowledge and teaching with the natural person's inability to receive and know the things of the Spirit. Chapter 3:1–4 continues by describing believers who are still carnal and behaving as men. That continuation prevents a simplistic equation of “spiritual” with flawless maturity.

### B. Argument commonly made

This passage is used to argue that the natural man cannot receive or know the things of the Spirit of God without spiritual enablement. The explicit language is strong: the natural man “receiveth not” these things, they are foolishness to him, and “neither can he know them,” because they are spiritually discerned.

The scope must remain precise. Paul is discussing “the things of the Spirit of God,” the wisdom God has revealed through the Spirit, and the message of Christ crucified in the context. He is not saying that an unregenerate person cannot learn ordinary facts, reason about created things, make choices, or understand any sentence about religion. The inability stated here concerns reception and discernment of the things of God's Spirit.

The passage also does not, by itself, define every faculty of the human will or give a complete chronology of regeneration and faith. It does establish that the natural person's problem is not merely a shortage of neutral information: the things of the Spirit are foolishness to him, and he cannot know them by the mode of discernment Paul describes. The Spirit is the source of revelation and the one who teaches.

### C. Immediate context and argument

**1 Corinthians 1:18–25 — the word of the cross.** Paul contrasts the preaching of the cross, which is foolishness to those who perish, with the power of God to those who are saved. God's wisdom is not measured by the world's standards. This establishes the conflict between human/worldly evaluation and God's revealed wisdom.

**1 Corinthians 1:26–31 — God's calling and boasting.** Paul reminds the believers that not many were wise, mighty, or noble according to the flesh. God chose what the world regards as foolish and weak, so that no flesh should glory in His presence. The stated result is that boasting is in the Lord. This is relevant to divine initiative and human boasting, but the passage should not be made to answer every question about the mechanics of the will.

**1 Corinthians 2:1–5 — Paul's proclamation.** Paul did not come with excellency of speech or worldly wisdom; he determined to know Jesus Christ and Him crucified. His speech and preaching were in demonstration of the Spirit and power, so that faith would not stand in the wisdom of men but in the power of God. The source and character of the message are central.

**1 Corinthians 2:6–9 — God's hidden wisdom.** Paul speaks wisdom among the mature, not the wisdom of this world or its rulers. God's wisdom was ordained before the world unto glory, and the rulers did not know it, as shown by their crucifixion of the Lord of glory. The quotation concerning what eye has not seen, nor ear heard, nor entered into the heart of man, is followed by God's revelation through the Spirit in verse 10. Do not detach verse 9 from verse 10 and imply that the revealed things remain unknowable to believers.

**1 Corinthians 2:10–11 — revelation belongs to the Spirit.** God reveals the things in view through His Spirit. The Spirit searches all things, including the deep things of God. Paul uses an analogy: a man's own spirit knows the things of the man; correspondingly, the things of God are known by the Spirit of God. The point is the Spirit's unique knowledge, not a claim that the Holy Spirit must search because He lacks knowledge.

**1 Corinthians 2:12 — reception of the Spirit.** “We have received” the Spirit which is of God, not the spirit of the world, so that believers might know the things freely given by God. The verse links reception of the Spirit with knowing God's gifts. It does not say that unaided natural human reasoning can produce this spiritual knowledge.

**1 Corinthians 2:13 — Spirit-taught communication.** Paul says that the things revealed are spoken not in words taught by man's wisdom but in words taught by the Holy Ghost, “comparing spiritual things with spiritual.” The final phrase has a debated translation and interpretation: it may mean explaining/interpreting spiritual things with spiritual words, or combining spiritual things with spiritual people. The core contrast is clear: the communication is Spirit-taught, not derived from merely human wisdom. The precise syntax should remain qualified.

**1 Corinthians 2:14 — the natural man.** The natural man does not receive the things of the Spirit of God. They are foolishness to him, and he cannot know them because they are spiritually discerned. The causal “because” matters: the limitation is tied to the spiritual mode of discernment required. The verse concerns these spiritual things, not every possible form of human knowledge or every exercise of choice.

**1 Corinthians 2:15–16 — the spiritual person and the mind of Christ.** The spiritual person discerns all things, while not being rightly assessed by the standards of the natural/worldly person. Paul then cites the question of who has known the mind of the Lord so as to instruct Him, and declares, “we have the mind of Christ.” This is not a claim that every believer possesses exhaustive divine knowledge. It identifies the believers' access to and orientation by Christ's revealed mind through the Spirit.

**1 Corinthians 3:1–4 — immature believers.** Paul says he could not speak to the Corinthians as spiritual but as carnal, as babes in Christ, because of envy, strife, and divisions. Thus “spiritual” in chapter 2 must not be treated as a synonym for sinless, fully mature, or incapable of error. A believer may have received the Spirit and yet require rebuke and growth.

### D. Original-language findings

- **ἀπεκάλυψεν (*apekalypsen*)**, “hath revealed” (v. 10): aorist active indicative, third person singular, from ἀποκαλύπτω (*apokalyptō*), “reveal, disclose, uncover.” God is the subject. The revelation is God's act, and it is “by his Spirit.”
- **διὰ τοῦ πνεύματος (*dia tou pneumatos*)**, “by his Spirit” (v. 10): διά (*dia*) with the genitive commonly expresses agency or means. The Spirit is identified as the agent/means through whom God reveals the things in view.
- **ἐραυνᾷ (*erauna*)**, “searcheth” (v. 10): present active indicative, third person singular, from ἐραυνάω (*eraunaō*), “search, examine, investigate.” The verse describes the Spirit's comprehensive knowledge, including the deep things of God. It must not be used to imply ignorance or acquisition of information by the omniscient Spirit.
- **οὐδεὶς ἔγνωκεν (*oudeis egnōken*)**, “no man knoweth” (v. 11): the perfect form of γινώσκω (*ginōskō*) is used in the analogy concerning knowledge of the things of God. The contrast identifies the Spirit of God as the one who knows God's things. The analogy should be read according to its stated point, not pressed into an exhaustive account of human and divine psychology.
- **ἐλάβομεν (*elabomen*)**, “we have received” (v. 12): aorist active indicative, first person plural, from λαμβάνω (*lambanō*), “receive, take.” Paul speaks of reception of the Spirit who is from God. The first-person plural refers to the Christian “we” in the argument; the precise scope of Paul's “we” (apostolic witnesses, believers more broadly, or both in the rhetorical flow) should be assessed contextually.
- **εἰδῶμεν (*eidōmen*)**, “that we might know” (v. 12): a form of οἶδα (*oida*), “know.” The purpose clause connects reception of the Spirit with knowing the things freely given by God.
- **ψυχικὸς ἄνθρωπος (*psychikos anthrōpos*)**, “natural man” (v. 14): ψυχικός (*psychikos*) is an adjective related to ψυχή (*psychē*), often rendered natural, unspiritual, or soulish according to context. It contrasts here with the spiritual person and identifies a person considered apart from the Spirit's discernment. The phrase should not be reduced to “a person with a body” or to a claim about ordinary human intelligence.
- **οὐ δέχεται (*ou dechetai*)**, “receiveth not” (v. 14): present middle indicative, third person singular, from δέχομαι (*dechomai*), “receive, accept, welcome.” The natural person does not welcome or accept the things of the Spirit of God. The verb expresses reception/acceptance, not merely failure to hear the words.
- **μωρία (*mōria*)**, “foolishness” (v. 14): the things of the Spirit are regarded as folly by the natural man. This indicates an evaluative/dispositional dimension, not merely an absent data point.
- **οὐ δύναται γνῶναι (*ou dynatai gnōnai*)**, “neither can he know” (v. 14): δύναται (*dynatai*) is present middle/deponent indicative, third person singular, from δύναμαι (*dynamai*), “be able, be capable”; γνῶναι (*gnōnai*) is an aorist active infinitive from γινώσκω (*ginōskō*), “know.” This is explicit inability concerning the things of the Spirit in view. It must neither be weakened into mere unwillingness nor expanded into universal inability to know anything.
- **πνευματικῶς ἀνακρίνεται (*pneumatikōs anakrinetai*)**, “they are spiritually discerned” (v. 14): πνευματικῶς means spiritually/by the Spirit; ἀνακρίνεται is present middle/passive-form indicative, third person singular, from ἀνακρίνω (*anakrinō*), “examine, evaluate, discern.” The things of the Spirit require spiritual discernment. The form and context concern the means or character of discernment, not a neutral test that the natural man can perform independently.
- **ὁ πνευματικός (*ho pneumatikos*)**, “he that is spiritual” (v. 15): the substantivized adjective “spiritual,” contrasted with the ψυχικός of verse 14. The contrast is in relation to the Spirit and the discernment of God's things. Chapter 3 shows that believers can be carnal/immature in conduct; therefore “spiritual” here must not be equated with sinless perfection.
- **ἀνακρίνει (*anakrinei*)**, “judgeth” (v. 15): present active indicative from ἀνακρίνω, “examine, evaluate, discern.” It is the same word family as the discernment in verse 14. The spiritual person can evaluate all things in the relevant context, while not being rightly evaluated by the natural person's standards. “All things” must be read in context and not inflated into omniscience.
- **νοῦν Χριστοῦ (*noun Christou*)**, “mind of Christ” (v. 16): νοῦς (*nous*) denotes mind, understanding, or outlook. Paul concludes with the declaration that “we have the mind of Christ,” in contrast to the inability of the natural man to know God's mind. This is a shared Christian possession through the Spirit and apostolic revelation, not a claim that believers become omniscient or equal to Christ.

### E. Canonical comparisons and counter-texts

**John 3:3–8.** Jesus says a man must be born again/of the Spirit to see and enter the kingdom of God. This is relevant to spiritual perception and the Spirit's work. The passage should be examined in its own terms; 1 Corinthians 2:14 is not a substitute for the new-birth language in John 3.

**John 6:44, 63–65.** Jesus says no man can come to Him except the Father draw him, and that the Spirit quickens while the flesh profits nothing. He also says that coming is given by the Father. This is a direct comparison for inability and divine enablement, but the wording and context of John 6 must be separately handled.

**Romans 8:5–11.** Paul contrasts the mind set on the flesh with the mind set on the Spirit, including the statement that the carnal mind is enmity against God and is not subject to God's law, “neither indeed can be.” This is a priority next entry (HW-012), not a conclusion to import prematurely into 1 Corinthians 2.

**Ephesians 2:1–10.** Paul describes those dead in trespasses and sins as made alive by God. This is relevant to spiritual life and divine initiative, but the metaphor of death should not be used to erase every form of human agency; the precise scope is established by Paul's own argument.

**James 1:18.** God begets believers of His own will with/in connection with the Word of Truth. This is consistent with the divine source of spiritual life, while 1 Corinthians 2 specifically addresses revelation and discernment.

**Philippians 2:12–13.** God works in believers both to will and to do, while believers are commanded to obey. This is relevant to divine operation and real human conduct, but it addresses believers' ongoing life rather than directly defining the natural man's state.

**1 Corinthians 3:1–4.** This is an essential internal qualification: people who are “in Christ” can be carnal and immature. Spiritual reception and possession of the Spirit do not mean every believer is fully mature in conduct or beyond correction.

### F. What the passage establishes

| Category | Finding | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| God reveals the things in view | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “God hath revealed them unto us by his Spirit” (v. 10). |
| The Spirit knows the deep things of God | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | The Spirit searches all things, including God's deep things (vv. 10–11). |
| Reception of the Spirit is connected with knowing God's gifts | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | The Spirit is received so believers might know what God freely gives (v. 12). |
| Spirit-taught communication | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | Paul says the things are spoken in words taught by the Holy Ghost (v. 13). |
| Natural man does not receive the things of the Spirit | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | Direct statement in verse 14. |
| Natural man regards them as foolishness | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | Direct statement in verse 14. |
| Natural man cannot know them by spiritual discernment | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “Neither can he know them, because they are spiritually discerned” (v. 14). |
| The inability concerns all human knowledge whatsoever | NOT ESTABLISHED | The stated object is the things of the Spirit of God. |
| The passage defines every faculty of the human will | NOT ESTABLISHED | It addresses reception, evaluation, and spiritual knowledge, not an exhaustive anthropology. |
| The natural man's problem is merely lack of information | NOT ADEQUATE TO THE TEXT | Paul describes non-reception, an evaluation of foolishness, and inability to know. |
| The natural man cannot make ordinary choices or understand ordinary facts | NOT ESTABLISHED | These are outside the stated scope. |
| Every believer is spiritually mature or sinless | CONTRADICTED BY THE LETTER'S CONTINUATION | 1 Corinthians 3:1–4 calls believers carnal and immature. |
| Believers possess exhaustive divine knowledge | NOT ESTABLISHED | “Mind of Christ” concerns spiritual understanding, not omniscience. |
| Complete chronology of regeneration and faith | NOT SETTLED | The passage establishes Spirit-dependent revelation and discernment but does not provide a full chronology. |

### G. Triple Constraint disposition

- **Biblical compliance — PASS, within scope.** The reading preserves God's revelation through the Spirit, the Spirit's unique knowledge, the natural man's non-reception and inability concerning the things of the Spirit, and the spiritual person's discernment. It also respects the context in chapters 1–3 and does not make the passage teach universal intellectual incapacity.
- **Scientific coherence — PASS, within scope.** The grammatical claims distinguish reception (δέχομαι), ability (δύναμαι), knowledge (γινώσκω), and discernment (ἀνακρίνω). The conclusion is limited to the text's stated object. No empirical or neurological claims are inferred from Paul's theological language.
- **GUA-W consistency — PASS, within scope.** God is the revealer; the Spirit is the one who knows and teaches; spiritual knowledge is received rather than independently authored by the natural person. Human reasoning and ordinary agency are not denied. No GUA-W architectural modification is proposed.

### H. Entry finding and limits

Paul describes a definite limitation in the natural man's relation to the things of the Spirit of God. The natural man does not receive them, regards them as foolishness, and cannot know them because they are spiritually discerned. This is stronger than saying that the person simply has not yet encountered enough information. The passage locates the relevant knowledge within God's revelation by the Spirit and the Spirit's teaching.

The scope must remain disciplined. Paul does not say that the natural man cannot know ordinary facts, reason about creation, or make choices. Nor does he define every aspect of human will. The passage does not independently establish the exact chronology of regeneration and faith. The continuation in 1 Corinthians 3 also prevents equating “spiritual” with sinless maturity: believers can be carnal and require correction.

**Finding:** 1 Corinthians 2:10–16 explicitly teaches that reception and discernment of the things of the Spirit depend upon God's revelation and the Spirit's teaching. The natural man, as contrasted with the spiritual person in this context, does not receive those things and cannot know them by spiritual discernment. The passage does not establish universal human intellectual inability or supply an exhaustive definition of the human will.

**Status:** PASS for Spirit-dependent revelation and discernment; PASS for the stated inability of the natural man concerning the things of the Spirit; FAIL for reducing the problem to mere lack of information; FAIL for expanding the verse into inability to know any ordinary truth or make any choice; UNRESOLVED for the complete relation of this inability to regeneration, faith, and the chronology of conversion.

### I. Related GUA-W recognition and cross-references

This passage witnesses a distinction between the Source of revelation and the recipient's capacity to discern what is revealed. God reveals; the Spirit knows and teaches; believers receive the Spirit and know the gifts of God; the natural man does not receive or discern the things of the Spirit. This is a textual recognition only, not a proposal to create or modify GUA-W architecture.

Cross-references for continued examination:
- John 3:3–8 — birth of the Spirit and seeing/entering the kingdom;
- John 6:44, 63–65 — inability to come, drawing, quickening, and what is given;
- Romans 8:5–11 — mind of the flesh, inability, and the Spirit's indwelling;
- Ephesians 2:1–10 — death in trespasses and God's quickening;
- 1 Corinthians 3:1–4 — carnal believers and spiritual immaturity.

### J. Research queue

1. HW-012 — Romans 8:5–11: examine flesh, Spirit, inability, indwelling, and life.
2. Retain John 3:3–8 and John 6:44, 63–65 as priority comparisons for spiritual perception, inability, and divine action.
3. Retain 1 Corinthians 3:1–4 as an internal control against equating spiritual status with sinless maturity.
4. Retain Acts 13:44–48 and 1 John 5:1 for later examination of belief, appointment, and birth of God.
5. Keep U-006 (definition and components of human will) open. This passage establishes a specific inability in spiritual reception and discernment but does not define every aspect of human willing.

---


### Canonical completion review — 2026-11-18

**Original limitation reviewed:** ** The scope of the natural person's inability, and the relation of Spirit-given understanding to conversion and faith, need contextual controls.

**Canonical passages examined:** **1 Corinthians 1:18–2:16; John 14:16–26; 16:12–15; Romans 8:5–11 (HW-012); Ephesians 1:17–19; 1 John 2:20, 27.**

**Combined biblical finding:** Paul's argument concerns God's wisdom revealed through the Spirit, not an inability to understand every ordinary fact. John 14 and 16 describe the Spirit's teaching and guidance. Romans 8 addresses the fleshly mind's inability to submit to God's law and the Spirit-indwelt contrast. Ephesians and First John speak of spiritual understanding and the Spirit's teaching.

**Revised disposition:** **NARROWED.** The inability is specifically about receiving and discerning the things of the Spirit in Paul's argument. The wider testimony supports the Spirit's teaching and illumination. It does not establish universal intellectual incapacity or an exhaustive theory of every stage of regeneration and faith.

**Synchronization instruction:** This disposition supplements the original passage analysis and governs the classification of the limitation above. Where an earlier table says only “not established by this passage alone” or “unresolved,” read that statement as a limit of the assigned passage, not as a conclusion about the whole canon. The cited passages must remain in their own contexts. Any remaining question is limited to the precise boundary stated here; no duplicate HW investigation is created.

## 18. Entry — HW-012: Romans 8:5–11 (2026-11-18)

### A. Exact KJV passage

> **Romans 8:5–11**
>
> 5 For they that are after the flesh do mind the things of the flesh; but they that are after the Spirit the things of the Spirit.
>
> 6 For to be carnally minded is death; but to be spiritually minded is life and peace.
>
> 7 Because the carnal mind is enmity against God: for it is not subject to the law of God, neither indeed can be.
>
> 8 So then they that are in the flesh cannot please God.
>
> 9 But ye are not in the flesh, but in the Spirit, if so be that the Spirit of God dwell in you. Now if any man have not the Spirit of Christ, he is none of his.
>
> 10 And if Christ be in you, the body is dead because of sin; but the Spirit is life because of righteousness.
>
> 11 But if the Spirit of him that raised up Jesus from the dead dwell in you, he that raised up Christ from the dead shall also quicken your mortal bodies by his Spirit that dwelleth in you.

The passage belongs to Paul's larger argument beginning in Romans 5 and developed through Romans 8. Its immediate background includes Romans 7:14–25, where Paul describes the conflict involving sin, the law, and the person who delights in God's law inwardly yet encounters the power of sin. Romans 8:1–4 announces no condemnation for those in Christ Jesus and describes God's action through His Son and the Spirit, where the law was weak through the flesh. Verses 12–17 then draw out the believers' obligation not to live after the flesh and describe life by the Spirit, adoption, and the Spirit's witness.

### B. Argument commonly made

Romans 8:7–8 is used to establish that the mind set on the flesh is hostile to God, does not submit to God's law, and cannot do so; those who are in the flesh cannot please God. The language is explicit about inability. The text does not describe a merely neutral person who needs only additional information or a stronger independent decision.

The subject and scope must be kept precise. Verse 7 speaks of the φρόνημα (*phronēma*) of the flesh — the mindset or orientation belonging to the flesh. Verse 8 speaks of those who are “in the flesh.” Paul does not say that the physical body as created by God is intrinsically evil, nor that an unregenerate person is incapable of every ordinary decision, civic good, intellectual task, or outwardly beneficial action. The inability in view concerns submission to God's law and pleasing God in the spiritual condition Paul describes.

Verses 9–11 contrast this condition with those in whom the Spirit of God dwells. Their identity is described by the Spirit's indwelling, their relation to Christ, and God's promise to give life to their mortal bodies. The passage therefore places the decisive distinction not in autonomous human self-reformation but in whether a person is in the flesh or in the Spirit, with the Spirit of God dwelling in him.

This passage does not, by itself, give an exhaustive account of every faculty of human willing or a complete chronology of regeneration, faith, and indwelling. It does expressly deny that the fleshly mindset can submit to God's law by its own capacity.

### C. Immediate context and argument

**Romans 7:14–25 — sin and the divided experience.** Paul describes the law as spiritual and the person as carnal, sold under sin. He describes doing what he hates and failing to do the good he would. He delights in the law of God after the inward man, yet sees another law in his members bringing him into captivity to the law of sin. The identity and scope of the “I” in this section have been debated; interpretations include Paul's experience under the law, a representative person, or a description with autobiographical dimensions. Romans 8 must be read as Paul's answer to the conflict and deliverance problem, not as an isolated statement about human psychology.

**Romans 8:1–4 — God's action in Christ.** There is no condemnation to those in Christ Jesus. The law of the Spirit of life in Christ Jesus has made believers free from the law of sin and death. What the law could not do because it was weak through the flesh, God did by sending His own Son. The righteous requirement of the law is fulfilled in those who walk not after the flesh but after the Spirit. The initiative in the decisive rescue is attributed to God.

**Romans 8:5 — two orientations.** Those who are according to the flesh mind the things of the flesh; those according to the Spirit mind the things of the Spirit. Paul describes contrasting orientations and their characteristic concerns. “Mind” is not merely momentary thought; it concerns the governing outlook or set of the person.

**Romans 8:6 — the outcomes.** The mindset of the flesh is death; the mindset of the Spirit is life and peace. The contrast is not simply between two equally acceptable lifestyle preferences. Paul places the orientations in relation to death and life, and the peace associated with God.

**Romans 8:7 — hostility and inability.** The mindset of the flesh is enmity against God. Paul explains: it is not subject to God's law, “neither indeed can be.” The inability is explicit and concerns submission to God's law. The text does not portray this mindset as spiritually neutral or as capable of overcoming its hostility through an unaided act of autonomous will.

**Romans 8:8 — inability to please God.** “They that are in the flesh cannot please God.” Paul moves from the mindset in verse 7 to the people characterized as being in the flesh. The statement is broad and categorical concerning their spiritual condition and pleasing God. It must not be weakened into mere difficulty, but neither should it be inflated into a claim that such persons cannot perform any outwardly useful or socially good act.

**Romans 8:9 — the defining contrast.** Paul tells the Roman believers that they are not in the flesh but in the Spirit, if the Spirit of God dwells in them. Anyone who does not have the Spirit of Christ does not belong to Him. The contrast is not presented as a self-generated upgrade of the fleshly mindset; the Spirit's indwelling marks belonging to Christ. “Spirit of God” and “Spirit of Christ” are both used in this one verse and must be retained in the reading.

**Romans 8:10 — body and Spirit.** If Christ is in the believer, the body is dead because of sin, but the Spirit is life because of righteousness. “Spirit” may be understood with reference to the Holy Spirit, or with an emphasis on the believer's spirit made alive by the Spirit; interpreters discuss the precise nuance. Verse 11's reference to the Spirit dwelling in believers and giving life to mortal bodies is an important control. The verse does not teach that the physical body is inherently evil or that bodily existence is discarded.

**Romans 8:11 — resurrection life.** The Spirit of the One who raised Jesus from the dead dwells in believers. The same God who raised Christ will give life to their mortal bodies through His indwelling Spirit. The future bodily dimension matters: salvation is not merely a change in mental attitude or an escape from material existence. God's Spirit is the agent associated with resurrection life.

**Romans 8:12–17 — obligation and adoption.** Paul concludes that believers are debtors, not to the flesh, to live after it. If they live after the flesh they shall die; if through the Spirit they mortify the deeds of the body, they shall live. Those led by the Spirit are sons of God; they have received the Spirit of adoption and cry, “Abba, Father.” The Spirit bears witness with their spirit. The Spirit's work does not cancel real action: believers are called to put to death the deeds of the body, but the means is “through the Spirit.”

### D. Original-language findings

- **κατὰ σάρκα (*kata sarka*)**, “after the flesh” (v. 5): κατά (*kata*) with the accusative expresses according to, in accordance with, or in the sphere of. σάρξ (*sarx*) means flesh. In Romans 8 it commonly refers to fallen human existence and orientation apart from the Spirit, not simply to physical tissue or the body as God's creation. Context determines the precise force.
- **φρονοῦσιν (*phronousin*)**, “do mind” (v. 5): present active indicative, third person plural, from φρονέω (*phroneō*), “think, set one's mind on, be minded toward.” The present form describes characteristic orientation. The contrast concerns the governing concerns of those characterized by flesh or Spirit.
- **τὸ φρόνημα (*to phronēma*)**, “to be carnally minded / the carnal mind” (vv. 6–7): a substantivized noun referring to mindset, outlook, or orientation. In verse 7, “the φρόνημα of the flesh” is the subject of the statement that it is enmity against God and is not subject to His law. It is not a technical synonym for every operation of the human brain or every faculty of the will.
- **θάνατος (*thanatos*)**, “death” (v. 6): death. In the contrast with “life and peace,” it has the force of the death-bound condition and outcome associated with the fleshly mindset. The wider argument includes both present spiritual condition and final destiny; the verse should not be restricted to physical death alone.
- **ζωὴ καὶ εἰρήνη (*zōē kai eirēnē*)**, “life and peace” (v. 6): life and peace are associated with the mindset of the Spirit. The terms are theological and relational in context, not merely biological vitality or temporary emotional calm.
- **ἔχθρα εἰς θεόν (*echthra eis theon*)**, “enmity against God” (v. 7): ἔχθρα (*echthra*) denotes hostility or enmity. The fleshly mindset is not neutral toward God; Paul explicitly characterizes it as hostile.
- **οὐχ ὑποτάσσεται (*ouch hypotassetai*)**, “it is not subject” (v. 7): present middle/passive-form indicative, third person singular, from ὑποτάσσω (*hypotassō*), “subject, subordinate, submit.” The fleshly mindset does not submit to God's law. The present form describes its characteristic relation; the grammar does not itself identify every possible cause or chronology of that condition.
- **οὐδὲ γὰρ δύναται (*oude gar dynatai*)**, “neither indeed can be” (v. 7): δύναται (*dynatai*) is present middle/deponent indicative, third person singular, from δύναμαι (*dynamai*), “be able, have power, be capable.” This is an explicit statement of inability. The subject remains the mindset of the flesh; the stated inability is to submit to God's law. It cannot be reduced to mere reluctance, but its object must not be expanded beyond Paul's words.
- **οἱ ἐν σαρκὶ ὄντες (*hoi en sarki ontes*)**, “they that are in the flesh” (v. 8): a substantival participial expression, “those being in the flesh.” Paul speaks of people characterized by this sphere or condition, not simply of people possessing physical bodies.
- **οὐ δύνανται (*ou dynantai*)**, “cannot” (v. 8): present middle/deponent indicative, third person plural, from δύναμαι. The people in the flesh are unable to please God. The statement concerns pleasing God in the theological context, not every form of outward human usefulness or social conduct.
- **ἀρέσαι (*aresai*)**, “to please” (v. 8): aorist active infinitive from ἀρέσκω (*areskō*), “please, be pleasing to.” The inability is directed to pleasing God. The aorist infinitive does not by itself specify a one-time event; it presents the action as a whole.
- **ἐν πνεύματι (*en pneumati*)**, “in the Spirit” (v. 9): ἐν with the dative, contrasted with “in the flesh.” In this context the reference is to the sphere/condition marked by the indwelling Holy Spirit. Paul immediately identifies the Spirit of God dwelling in believers and then the Spirit of Christ.
- **οἰκεῖ (*oikei*)**, “dwell” (v. 9): present active indicative, third person singular, from οἰκέω (*oikeō*), “dwell, reside.” The Spirit's indwelling is the stated condition in Paul's contrast. The present form depicts the Spirit as dwelling in believers; it does not by itself settle every question about the timing of indwelling relative to faith.
- **εἴ τις πνεῦμα Χριστοῦ οὐκ ἔχει (*ei tis pneuma Christou ouk echei*)**, “if any man have not the Spirit of Christ” (v. 9): Paul states that the person who does not have the Spirit of Christ does not belong to Christ. “Spirit of God” and “Spirit of Christ” are placed in parallel in the verse. The text's identification is important and should not be fragmented into competing spirits.
- **νεκρόν (*nekron*)**, “dead” (v. 10): the body is described as dead because of sin. This points to mortality and the body subject to death; it does not teach that embodiment itself is evil.
- **πνεῦμα ζωὴ (*pneuma zōē*)**, “the Spirit is life” (v. 10): the syntax permits discussion of whether πνεῦμα refers directly to the Holy Spirit or to the believer's spirit in the life given by the Holy Spirit. Verse 11's explicit indwelling Spirit and the argument as a whole must govern interpretation. Do not overstate a disputed nuance.
- **ζῳοποιήσει (*zōopoiēsei*)**, “shall also quicken” (v. 11): future active indicative, third person singular, from ζῳοποιέω (*zōopoieō*), “make alive, give life.” God will give life to believers' mortal bodies. The future form points to the promised life-giving action; the verse connects it to the resurrection of Jesus and the Spirit's indwelling.
- **διὰ τοῦ ἐνοικοῦντος αὐτοῦ πνεύματος (*dia tou enoikountos autou pneumatos*)**, “by his Spirit that dwelleth in you” (v. 11): διά with the genitive indicates means or agency; ἐνοικοῦντος is a present active participle from ἐνοικέω, “dwell in.” The Spirit's indwelling is connected with God's future life-giving of mortal bodies.

### E. Canonical comparisons and counter-texts

**Romans 7:14–25.** The immediate preceding account of sin's power and the conflict with the law is essential context. The precise identity and rhetorical function of the “I” in that passage remain debated; HW-012 should not settle that debate by assumption.

**Romans 8:1–4.** God does what the law could not do because it was weak through the flesh. This is the immediate explanation for the deliverance and new condition described in chapter 8. God's action in sending His Son and condemning sin in the flesh must remain central.

**Romans 8:12–17.** Believers are commanded to mortify the deeds of the body through the Spirit. This preserves real human action while identifying the Spirit as the means. It is an internal control against treating divine indwelling as passivity.

**John 6:44, 63–65.** Jesus says no man can come unless the Father draw him, and that the Spirit quickens. These are direct comparisons for inability and divine action. Their precise relation to Romans 8 should be assessed without merging distinct contexts.

**1 Corinthians 2:10–16.** The natural man does not receive the things of the Spirit and cannot know them because they are spiritually discerned. Romans 8 adds the language of hostility to God, inability to submit to His law, and inability to please Him.

**Ephesians 2:1–10.** Those dead in trespasses and sins are made alive by God. This is a relevant parallel for divine quickening and the transition from death to life, while preserving the distinct imagery and argument of Ephesians.

**Galatians 5:16–25.** Paul contrasts the works of the flesh and fruit of the Spirit and calls believers to walk in the Spirit. This further preserves both the conflict and the believer's commanded conduct.

**Philippians 2:12–13.** God works in believers both to will and to do, while they are commanded to work out their salvation. This concerns the Spirit-enabled life of believers and should not be used to bypass the specific inability Paul assigns to the fleshly mindset in Romans 8.

### F. What the passage establishes

| Category | Finding | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| Flesh and Spirit represent contrasting orientations/conditions | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | Verses 5–9 contrast those after/in the flesh with those after/in the Spirit. |
| The fleshly mindset is hostile to God | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | Verse 7 calls it enmity against God. |
| The fleshly mindset does not submit to God's law | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | Verse 7. |
| The fleshly mindset cannot submit to God's law | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “Neither indeed can be” (v. 7). |
| Those in the flesh cannot please God | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | Verse 8. |
| The inability concerns every ordinary human choice or action | NOT ESTABLISHED | Paul's stated objects are submission to God's law and pleasing God. |
| Physical embodiment is itself evil | NOT ESTABLISHED | “Flesh” in this argument is not simply the physical body; verses 10–11 affirm God's life-giving purpose for mortal bodies. |
| The Spirit's indwelling marks the believer's relation to Christ | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | Verses 9–11. |
| Believers have no responsibility to act | CONTRADICTED BY CONTEXT | Verses 12–13 command mortification of the deeds of the body through the Spirit. |
| The Spirit gives life to mortal bodies | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | Verse 11 connects God's resurrection power with the indwelling Spirit. |
| The passage provides a complete chronology of regeneration, faith, and indwelling | NOT SETTLED | The text establishes the contrast and indwelling condition but does not lay out every temporal step. |
| The passage exhaustively defines all faculties of human will | NOT ESTABLISHED | It addresses the fleshly mindset, submission, pleasing God, and Spirit-indwelt life. |

### G. Triple Constraint disposition

- **Biblical compliance — PASS, within scope.** The reading preserves the explicit inability of the fleshly mindset to submit to God's law and of those in the flesh to please God. It also preserves the Spirit's indwelling, the believer's responsibility to mortify sinful deeds through the Spirit, and the future quickening of mortal bodies.
- **Scientific coherence — PASS, within scope.** The Greek subjects and objects of the inability statements are identified precisely. The theological use of “flesh” is not confused with physical tissue, and no neurological or empirical claims are inferred from Paul's language. The grammatical forms are not used to invent a complete chronology.
- **GUA-W consistency — PASS, within scope.** The text attributes the decisive transition and life-giving work to God through Christ and the indwelling Spirit. Human action remains real but is not assigned independent originating authority over spiritual life. No GUA-W architectural modification is proposed.

### H. Entry finding and limits

Romans 8:5–11 explicitly describes the mindset of the flesh as hostile to God, not subject to His law, and unable to submit to it. Paul further states that those who are in the flesh cannot please God. This is not merely a statement that such people lack sufficient information or need to make a stronger unaided decision. The inability is stated directly.

The scope must remain exact. Verse 7's subject is the mindset of the flesh; verse 8 speaks of those who are in the flesh. The passage does not say that a person outside Christ cannot perform any ordinary action or outwardly beneficial deed. Nor does it condemn the physical body as God's creation. Verses 10–11 look toward the giving of life to mortal bodies through the Spirit who dwells in believers.

The contrast in verses 9–11 is marked by the Spirit of God dwelling in the believer and the person's belonging to Christ. Verses 12–13 then preserve the believer's real obligation and action, carried out through the Spirit. The text does not provide an exhaustive account of the human will or a complete chronology of regeneration, faith, and indwelling.

**Finding:** The fleshly mindset is explicitly hostile to God, does not submit to God's law, and cannot do so. Those in the flesh cannot please God. The believer's contrasting condition is marked by the indwelling Spirit of God/Christ, and the Spirit is associated with life and the future quickening of mortal bodies. The passage does not establish universal incapacity for ordinary human activity, nor does it define every faculty of the will.

**Status:** PASS for the stated inability of the fleshly mindset to submit to God's law; PASS for the stated inability of those in the flesh to please God; PASS for the Spirit's indwelling and life-giving work; FAIL for reducing the inability to mere lack of information or reluctance; FAIL for treating the physical body as inherently evil or denying all ordinary human agency; UNRESOLVED for the complete chronology of regeneration, faith, and indwelling, and for an exhaustive definition of human will.

### I. Related GUA-W recognition and cross-references

This passage witnesses a distinction between the fleshly condition and the Spirit-indwelt condition. The fleshly mindset is hostile and unable to submit to God's law; the Spirit dwells in those who belong to Christ and is associated with life. The believer's action is real, but Romans 8:13 specifies that sinful deeds are put to death “through the Spirit.” This is a textual recognition, not a proposal to create or modify GUA-W architecture.

Relevant established distinctions:
- fleshly mindset versus Spirit-directed mindset;
- hostility versus submission;
- inability versus Spirit-enabled life;
- God's indwelling operation versus the believer's real obedience;
- mortal body versus the Spirit's promise of resurrection life.

### J. Research queue

1. HW-013 — Acts 13:44–48: examine belief, divine appointment, and the response to the word in Luke's narrative.
2. HW-014 — 1 John 5:1: examine the grammatical and contextual relation between believing and having been born of God.
3. Retain John 6:44, 63–65 as a priority comparison for inability, drawing, quickening, and what is given.
4. Retain Romans 7:14–25 for a separate contextual and interpretive examination of Paul's “I,” sin, law, and inability.
5. Keep U-006 (definition and components of human will) open. Romans 8 establishes a specific inability of the fleshly mindset but does not exhaustively define every aspect of human willing.

---


### Canonical completion review — 2026-11-18

**Original limitation reviewed:** ** The object and scope of the fleshly mind's inability, and its relation to indwelling, new birth, and future bodily life, need the wider Pauline and prophetic context.

**Canonical passages examined:** **Romans 7:14–25; 8:1–17, 18–27; Galatians 5:16–25; Ezekiel 36:26–27; John 3:5–8; 1 Corinthians 2:10–16 (HW-011).**

**Combined biblical finding:** Romans 7–8 supplies Paul's movement from the conflict under sin to life in the Spirit, adoption, and future bodily redemption. Galatians 5 contrasts works of the flesh with the fruit of the Spirit. Ezekiel 36 supplies the promised new heart and Spirit-enabled walk. John 3 identifies birth from the Spirit. First Corinthians 2 addresses Spirit-dependent discernment.

**Revised disposition:** **NARROWED.** The stated inability concerns submission to God's law and pleasing God, not every ordinary human action. The Spirit's indwelling and future quickening are explicit. The exact chronology of conversion and an exhaustive account of the will are not provided by these passages as a complete sequence.

**Synchronization instruction:** This disposition supplements the original passage analysis and governs the classification of the limitation above. Where an earlier table says only “not established by this passage alone” or “unresolved,” read that statement as a limit of the assigned passage, not as a conclusion about the whole canon. The cited passages must remain in their own contexts. Any remaining question is limited to the precise boundary stated here; no duplicate HW investigation is created.

## 19. Entry — HW-013: Acts 13:44–48 (2026-11-18)

### A. Exact KJV passage

> **Acts 13:44–48**
>
> 44 And the next sabbath day came almost the whole city together to hear the word of God.
>
> 45 But when the Jews saw the multitudes, they were filled with envy, and spake against those things which were spoken by Paul, contradicting and blaspheming.
>
> 46 Then Paul and Barnabas waxed bold, and said, It was necessary that the word of God should first have been spoken to you: but seeing ye put it from you, and judge yourselves unworthy of everlasting life, lo, we turn to the Gentiles.
>
