6. **The passive sealing is a human act.** The passive form presents the hearers as recipients, not as the agents sealing themselves. The Spirit is explicitly named in connection with the sealing.
7. **The grammar proves a lengthy gap between believing and sealing.** It does not. The aorist participles normally place the events before the main verb, but the events may be closely connected or coincident in conversion.
8. **The Spirit is only a symbol of a future inheritance.** Verse 14 identifies Him as the earnest/guarantee of the inheritance. The passage gives Him a present role as God's pledge.
9. **The KJV's supplied “trusted” is a separate Greek finite verb repeated in verse 13.** It is not. The Greek has the participles “having heard” and “having believed,” with “you were sealed” as the finite passive verb.
10. **Verse 11's “obtained an inheritance” is the only possible rendering.** The Greek verb ἐκληρώθημεν carries allotment/heritage language and has been translated in more than one way. Preserve the KJV while noting the range.
11. **The passage explains every detail of the mechanics of faith, sealing, or final redemption.** It does not. It identifies the events and their relation, but not every causal or chronological mechanism.
12. **The passage's “all things” means every individual is thereby saved.** The text describes the gathering of all things in Christ; it does not in these verses explicitly state universal individual salvation. Do not make that inference without broader examination of Paul's language and context.

### H. Canonical comparisons

- **Romans 8:28–30 (HW-016):** God's purpose, predestination, calling, justification, and glorification.
- **2 Thessalonians 2:13–14 (HW-015):** God's choice, sanctification of the Spirit, belief of truth, and calling through the Gospel.
- **Romans 10:13–17:** the relationship between preaching, hearing, and believing.
- **2 Corinthians 1:21–22:** God's establishing, anointing, sealing, and giving the Spirit as earnest.
- **Ephesians 4:30:** the Spirit's sealing unto the day of redemption.
- **Romans 8:23:** the future redemption of the body, relevant to the inheritance and redemption horizon.
- **John 6:37–65:** the Father's giving and drawing, the Son's reception, and coming to Christ.
- **Acts 10:44–48; 19:1–6:** compare actual narrative accounts of the Spirit's reception without forcing every narrative into a single chronology.
- **1 Peter 1:3–5:** new birth, living hope, inheritance, and God's preserving power.
- **2 Timothy 1:9:** salvation and calling according to God's purpose and grace before the world began.

### I. What the passage establishes

| Category | Finding | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| God is the subject of choosing in verse 4 | EXPLICIT | ἐξελέξατο |
| Choosing is in Christ | EXPLICIT | ἐν αὐτῷ |
| The choosing is before the foundation of the world | EXPLICIT | πρὸ καταβολῆς κόσμου |
| Predestination is unto adoption | EXPLICIT | Ephesians 1:5 |
| The purpose is according to God's good pleasure and will | EXPLICIT | Ephesians 1:5, 9, 11 |
| Redemption is in Christ through His blood | EXPLICIT | Ephesians 1:7 |
| God makes known the mystery of His will | EXPLICIT | Ephesians 1:9 |
| God's purpose includes gathering all things in Christ | EXPLICIT | Ephesians 1:10 |
| God works all things according to the counsel of His will | EXPLICIT | Ephesians 1:11 |
| The hearers heard the Gospel | EXPLICIT | ἀκούσαντες, 1:13 |
| The hearers believed | EXPLICIT | πιστεύσαντες, 1:13 |
| The believers were sealed | EXPLICIT | Passive ἐσφραγίσθητε |
| The Spirit is the earnest/guarantee of the inheritance | EXPLICIT | 1:14 |
| Active believing proves faith is the independent originating cause of election | NOT ESTABLISHED | Voice does not establish ultimate causal origin |
| The participles prove a long temporal gap before sealing | NOT ESTABLISHED | Aspect and construction do not specify such a gap |
| The Spirit is the agent or the seal in every technical grammatical sense | GRAMMATICAL QUESTION | Dative construction is discussed; the Spirit's association is explicit |
| “All things in Christ” proves universal individual salvation | NOT ESTABLISHED | Scope of the phrase does not itself state that conclusion |
| The passage explains every mechanism of faith and regeneration | NOT ESTABLISHED | It identifies the connected events, not every mechanism |

### J. Triple Constraint disposition

- **Biblical compliance — PASS, within scope.** Preserves God's choosing and purpose, the repeated Christ-reference, adoption, redemption through Christ's blood, Gospel hearing, believing, sealing, and the Spirit as guarantee. It does not erase human belief or make the passage answer questions it does not address.
- **Scientific coherence — PASS, within scope.** Distinguishes grammatical subjects, active participles, passive finite verb, tense/aspect, and interpretive uncertainty. No empirical claim is derived from the theological text.
- **GUA-W consistency — PASS, within scope.** The text distinguishes God's purpose, Christ's mediatorial work, Gospel proclamation, human believing, divine sealing, and promised inheritance. This is a textual recognition only; no architectural change or new Gear is proposed.

### K. Entry finding and limits

Ephesians 1:3–14 attributes the choosing, predestinating, purposing, revealing, and working described in the passage to God. The choosing is explicitly “in Christ” and before the foundation of the world. Predestination is directed toward adoption, and the passage's wider purpose is the gathering of all things in Christ. Redemption is through Christ's blood.

Verses 13–14 expressly include the hearers' hearing of the Gospel and believing. The verb “were sealed” is passive, presenting the believers as recipients of the sealing, and the promised Holy Spirit is identified as the earnest/guarantee of their inheritance. The KJV's first “trusted” in verse 13 is supplied in English; the Greek construction contains the participles “having heard” and “having believed” with “you were sealed” as the finite verb.

The active voice of believing does not establish that faith is the independent originating cause of God's choosing or purpose. Conversely, God's purpose does not make believing unreal or unnecessary in Paul's account. The precise temporal relation between believing and sealing is not fully settled by the aorist forms alone; the events may be sequential yet closely connected, or closely coincident in conversion. The passage does not explain every internal mechanism of faith, sealing, or final redemption.

**Finding:** Ephesians 1:3–14 presents God's purpose and action as foundational, Christ as the sphere and mediator of the blessings, redemption through Christ's blood, the Gospel as the message heard, human believing as an explicit response, and the Holy Spirit as the promised seal and guarantee of inheritance. The text neither makes human faith the independent originating cause of God's purpose nor erases faith from the saving account. The exact temporal relation of believing and sealing, the grammatical force of the Spirit's dative, and the full mechanics of faith remain matters for careful comparison.

**Status:** PASS for God's initiative and purpose; PASS for election and predestination being explicitly in Christ and directed toward adoption/holiness; PASS for the inclusion of Gospel hearing and human believing; PASS for the passive sealing and the Spirit as guarantee; UNRESOLVED for the precise temporal relation of faith and sealing, the full mechanics of faith, and the exact grammatical force of the Spirit's dative.

### L. GUA-W recognition and research queue

The passage witnesses distinct but connected roles: God's purpose and choosing; Christ as the one in whom the blessings and redemption are found; the Gospel as the message heard; human believing; the Spirit's sealing; and the inheritance guaranteed until redemption. These roles must not be collapsed into one another. This is a textual recognition, not a new GUA-W architectural rule.

Retain these guardrails:
- do not detach election from the repeated “in Christ” language;
- do not erase the stated purposes of holiness and adoption;
- do not turn active believing into proof of independent originating causality;
- do not erase hearing and believing from the passage;
- do not mistake the KJV's supplied “trusted” for a separate repeated Greek finite verb;
- do not claim a long gap between believing and sealing from the aorist forms alone;
- do not obscure the Spirit's identity as the promised seal/guarantee;
- do not claim universal individual salvation solely from “gather together all things in Christ”;
- retain the translation range of ἐκληρώθημεν in verse 11.

Next investigations:
1. HW-018 — 1 Peter 1:1–5: COMPLETED — see entry above. This passage is now investigated once; future findings belong in this entry.
2. Retain Romans 10:13–17 and 2 Corinthians 1:21–22 as cross-references within relevant existing entries, not as duplicate full investigations.
3. Keep U-006 (definition and components of human will) open; the completed entries do not yet exhaustively define every aspect of human willing. Ephesians 1 identifies believing as a real response but does not by itself exhaustively define its origin or every aspect of human willing.

---



### Canonical completion review — 2026-11-18

**Original limitation reviewed:** ** The relation between hearing, believing, sealing, election, and inheritance, including the timing of sealing, needs the wider canonical context.

**Canonical passages examined:** **Ephesians 2:1–10 (HW-008); 4:30; Romans 8:23, 28–30 (HW-016); 10:13–17; 2 Corinthians 1:21–22; Acts 10:34–48; 19:1–7; 1 Peter 1:1–5 (HW-018); 2 Thessalonians 2:13–14 (HW-015); 2 Timothy 1:9.**

**Combined biblical finding:** Ephesians 2 clarifies grace, faith, and works. Romans 10 clarifies preaching/hearing/believing. Second Corinthians 1 and Ephesians 4 describe sealing and the Spirit as earnest/guarantee toward redemption. Acts 10 and 19 provide distinct narrative cases concerning the Spirit's reception; they must not be forced into one invariant chronology. First Peter and Second Thessalonians provide further election/Spirit/belief comparisons.

**Revised disposition:** **NARROWED.** God's purpose, election in Christ, redemption, Gospel hearing, human believing, and the Spirit's sealing/guarantee are established. The exact temporal relation between believing and sealing and the complete mechanism by which faith arises remain not exhaustively specified.

**Synchronization instruction:** This disposition supplements the original passage analysis and governs the classification of the limitation above. Where an earlier table says only “not established by this passage alone” or “unresolved,” read that statement as a limit of the assigned passage, not as a conclusion about the whole canon. The cited passages must remain in their own contexts. Any remaining question is limited to the precise boundary stated here; no duplicate HW investigation is created.

## 24. Entry — HW-018: 1 Peter 1:1–5 (2026-11-18)

### A. Exact KJV passage

> **1 Peter 1:1–5**
>
> 1 Peter, an apostle of Jesus Christ, to the strangers scattered throughout Pontus, Galatia, Cappadocia, Asia, and Bithynia,
>
> 2 Elect according to the foreknowledge of God the Father, through sanctification of the Spirit, unto obedience and sprinkling of the blood of Jesus Christ: Grace unto you, and peace, be multiplied.
>
> 3 Blessed be the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, which according to his abundant mercy hath begotten us again unto a lively hope by the resurrection of Jesus Christ from the dead,
>
> 4 To an inheritance incorruptible, and undefiled, and that fadeth not away, reserved in heaven for you,
>
> 5 Who are kept by the power of God through faith unto salvation ready to be revealed in the last time.

### B. Immediate context — 1 Peter 1:1–12

The passage opens a letter to believers living as scattered sojourners in several regions of Asia Minor. Peter's greeting identifies them in relation to election, the Father's foreknowledge, the Spirit's sanctifying work, obedience, and the blood of Jesus Christ (vv. 1–2).

Verses 3–5 move from the greeting to praise of God. Peter attributes the new birth to God's abundant mercy, identifies the resurrection of Jesus Christ as the ground or means through which the living hope is given, describes the inheritance, and says that the recipients are guarded by God's power through faith.

Verses 6–9 continue by acknowledging present grief through manifold temptations and describing the testing and preciousness of faith, which has an appointed end in praise, honour, and glory at the appearing of Jesus Christ. Peter speaks of love for Christ, believing in Him though not now seen, and receiving the end of faith, the salvation of souls.

Verses 10–12 describe the prophets' inquiry into the salvation and grace now announced in the Gospel, and the Holy Ghost's role in the testimony concerning Christ. The assigned unit is verses 1–5; verses 6–12 supply immediate context, not a second investigation.

### C. Greek and grammatical findings

**Verse 1 — the recipients**

- **ἐκλεκτοῖς (*eklektois*)**, “elect”: dative plural adjective, agreeing with the recipients addressed. Peter identifies them as chosen/elect.
- **παρεπιδήμοις (*parepidēmois*)**, “strangers” or “sojourners”: dative plural, also describing the recipients. The KJV's “strangers scattered” conveys their status as dispersed sojourners; the expression should not be made to imply that every recipient was ethnically Jewish. The letter's identity language has both geographical and theological dimensions.

**Verse 2 — the description of election**

- **κατὰ πρόγνωσιν θεοῦ πατρός (*kata prognōsin Theou Patros*)**, “according to the foreknowledge of God the Father”: κατά with the accusative πρόγνωσιν expresses accordance with or according to foreknowledge. The genitive “of God the Father” identifies whose foreknowledge is in view. The clause does not grammatically say “according to the faith God foresaw,” nor does it name a human act as the ground of election.
- **ἐν ἁγιασμῷ πνεύματος (*en hagiasmō pneumatos*)**, rendered “through sanctification of the Spirit”: ἐν with the dative can express sphere or means. Peter places the election of these believers in relation to the Spirit's sanctifying work. The genitive πνεύματος identifies the Spirit in relation to sanctification; the precise nuance should not be forced beyond the construction.
- **εἰς ὑπακοὴν καὶ ῥαντισμὸν αἵματος Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ (*eis hypakoēn kai rhantismon haimatos Iēsou Christou*)**, “unto obedience and sprinkling of the blood of Jesus Christ”: εἰς marks the intended end, goal, or result. The two coordinated nouns are obedience and sprinkling. The phrase connects the elect recipients with obedience and the covenant/sacrificial imagery of Christ's blood. It does not identify autonomous human will as the source of election.
- The verse names the Father, the Spirit, and Jesus Christ in distinct relations to election, sanctification, obedience, and the blood. These relations should be preserved rather than collapsed into one undifferentiated action.

**Verse 3 — new birth and living hope**

- **εὐλογητός (*eulogētos*)**, “Blessed”: opens the doxology, directing praise to the God and Father of Jesus Christ.
- **κατὰ τὸ πολὺ αὐτοῦ ἔλεος (*kata to poly autou eleos*)**, “according to his abundant mercy”: the new birth is expressly related to God's abundant mercy.
- **ἀναγεννήσας (*anagennēsas*)**, “hath begotten us again”: aorist active participle, nominative masculine singular, from ἀναγεννάω (*anagennaō*). God is the subject; the believers are the recipients of the begetting. The active form attributes the begetting action to God. The aorist presents the action as a whole but does not, by itself, establish a detailed chronology between regeneration and the first act of faith.
- **εἰς ἐλπίδα ζῶσαν (*eis elpida zōsan*)**, “unto a lively [living] hope”: the new birth is directed toward living hope.
- **δι᾽ ἀναστάσεως Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ ἐκ νεκρῶν (*di’ anastaseōs Iēsou Christou ek nekrōn*)**, “by the resurrection of Jesus Christ from the dead”: διά with the genitive presents Christ's resurrection as the means or ground through which this living hope is given. The hope is not presented as a product of independent human self-origination.

**Verse 4 — the inheritance**

- **κληρονομίαν (*klēronomian*)**, “inheritance”: the promised inheritance is described by three negatives: incorruptible, undefiled, and unfading.
- **τετηρημένην (*tetērēmenēn*)**, “reserved”: perfect passive participle, feminine accusative singular, agreeing with “inheritance.” The perfect passive describes the inheritance as having been kept/reserved and remaining so. The clause does not name the agent in this participle, though the surrounding praise and divine action place the promise within God's provision.
- **ἐν οὐρανοῖς εἰς ὑμᾶς (*en ouranois eis hymas*)**, “in heaven for you”: identifies its location and intended recipients.

**Verse 5 — divine keeping through faith**

- **τοὺς ἐν δυνάμει θεοῦ φρουρουμένους (*tous en dynamei Theou phrouroumenous*)**, “who are kept by the power of God”: φρουρουμένους is a present passive participle, accusative masculine plural, describing the believers. The passive form presents them as those being guarded; “the power of God” identifies the divine power in which this guarding occurs.
- **διὰ πίστεως (*dia pisteōs*)**, “through faith”: διά with the genitive expresses means or mediation. Faith is not erased; it is explicitly included in the account of God's keeping. The text does not, in this phrase, identify faith as the independent originating cause of God's election, mercy, new birth, or power.
- **εἰς σωτηρίαν (*eis sōtērian*)**, “unto salvation”: marks the goal toward which the divine keeping through faith is directed.
- **ἀποκαλυφθῆναι (*apokalyphthēnai*)**, “to be revealed”: aorist passive infinitive, describing salvation as ready to be revealed in the last time. Peter's horizon includes a future unveiling; the passage does not reduce salvation to a merely present human decision.

The grammatical pattern is therefore significant: God is praised as the one whose abundant mercy begets believers again; the inheritance is kept; and the believers are described as being guarded in God's power through faith. Human faith is a real and necessary term in the sentence, but Peter does not identify it as the independent source of the saving action.

### D. The central interpretive question — “according to the foreknowledge of God the Father”

The expression **“according to the foreknowledge of God the Father”** must be read as written. It identifies God's foreknowledge as the reference according to which the recipients are elect. It does not explicitly state that God elected them because He foresaw an independently originating act of faith.

Three readings or emphases must be distinguished:

1. **Prior knowledge / prescience:** God knew beforehand. This is within the ordinary semantic range of foreknowledge. Some interpreters understand the foreknowledge in relation to God's prior knowledge of the persons and their future faith. The latter specification—“their future faith”—is an interpretive proposal, not an expressed object in this clause.
2. **Personal or relational foreknowledge:** God's prior knowledge of the people is understood relationally, not as bare awareness of future facts. This reading draws on broader biblical uses of knowing, but the noun πρόγνωσις does not, by itself, settle every element of a doctrine of forelove.
3. **Foreordination / prior purpose:** Some interpret foreknowledge in a stronger purposive sense, especially when reading this verse alongside other passages concerning God's prior purpose. That conclusion must be argued from the broader context and canonical usage; it should not be smuggled into the lexical definition without demonstration.

The text explicitly identifies God the Father as the one whose foreknowledge is in view and relates election to the Spirit's sanctification and the obedience and blood of Jesus Christ. It does not spell out the complete ground, mechanism, or chronology of election and faith. Those questions remain for comparison with other passages, not for an unsupported conclusion from one noun.

### E. The order of divine action and human response

The passage contains both divine initiative and genuine human response. Neither side may be removed.

**Divine action explicitly stated:**
- The recipients are elect according to the foreknowledge of God the Father (vv. 1–2).
- Their election is related to the sanctification of the Spirit (v. 2).
- God, according to abundant mercy, has begotten them again (v. 3).
- Their inheritance is described as reserved (v. 4).
- They are being guarded by God's power (v. 5).

**Human response or participation explicitly stated:**
- Election is unto obedience (v. 2).
- God's people are guarded through faith (v. 5).
- The immediate continuation identifies their believing in Christ and loving Him, though they do not now see Him (vv. 8–9).

The presence of faith does not make faith the originating source of the mercy and new birth Peter attributes to God. Conversely, God's initiative does not turn faith and obedience into unreal or irrelevant terms. Peter's construction holds them in relation: divine power guards believers through faith, and election is unto obedience.

The passage does not specify the complete temporal order between the Spirit's sanctification, new birth, and the first act of faith. The aorist participle “hath begotten us again” does not alone provide a measurable interval or a complete doctrine of conversion. The present passive participle “being kept” describes ongoing guarding; it does not by itself settle every question about perseverance or apostasy.

### F. Inheritance, assurance, and the future horizon

Peter's sequence moves from God's abundant mercy and new birth to living hope, inheritance, divine keeping, and future salvation. The resurrection of Jesus Christ is central to the living hope (v. 3). The inheritance is incorruptible, undefiled, and unfading (v. 4). God's power guards the believers through faith toward salvation to be revealed in the last time (v. 5).

This is not a claim that believers never experience suffering. Verses 6–7 immediately acknowledge grief and the testing of faith. Nor does the future horizon mean that salvation is absent in the present: Peter describes believers as already begotten again and presently guarded, while awaiting the revelation of salvation's fullness.

The passage's assurance rests on God's mercy, Christ's resurrection, the promised inheritance, and God's power. It does not rest on a claim that human beings independently preserve or originate the saving order.

### G. Canonical comparisons — comparison only, not duplicate investigations

- **Romans 8:28–30 (HW-016):** Both passages attribute decisive saving actions and purpose to God. Romans 8 names foreknowledge, predestination, calling, justification, and glorification; 1 Peter 1:2 names election according to the Father's foreknowledge. Neither passage explicitly says that election is grounded in a self-originated human faith.
- **Ephesians 1:3–14 (HW-017):** Both passages place election in relation to God's purpose and include human response. Ephesians explicitly records hearing and believing before the sealing described in verse 13; 1 Peter explicitly places obedience and faith within the saving account. These passages complement one another without making faith the originating cause of God's purpose.
- **2 Thessalonians 2:13–14 (HW-015):** Paul joins God's choice unto salvation with sanctification of the Spirit and belief of the truth, and then identifies the Gospel as the means of calling. This is a close comparison to 1 Peter 1:2, but it remains the already-completed HW-015 entry.
- **Romans 10:13–17:** Paul's account of calling upon the Lord, believing, hearing, and preaching supplies a related account of human response and Gospel proclamation. It should be cross-referenced where relevant, not opened as a duplicate investigation of Romans 9–11.
- **2 Timothy 1:9:** Retain as a future comparison concerning God's saving and calling according to His own purpose and grace. Do not assign a new investigation number until the repository has been checked for any existing treatment of the exact passage.

These are canonical cross-references only. They do not create additional HW investigations or alter the passage boundaries of HW-015, HW-016, or HW-017.

### H. What the passage establishes — and what it does not

| Question | Finding | Textual basis / limit |
|---|---|---|
| Are the recipients identified as elect? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | 1 Peter 1:1–2. |
| Is election related to the Father's foreknowledge? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “According to the foreknowledge of God the Father” (v. 2). |
| Does verse 2 say that God elected them because He foresaw their faith? | NOT EXPLICITLY STATED | Faith is not named as the object or ground of foreknowledge in this clause. |
| Is the Spirit's sanctifying work included? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “Through sanctification of the Spirit” (v. 2). |
| Is obedience included in the purpose or end of election? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “Unto obedience” (v. 2). |
| Is Christ's blood included? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “Sprinkling of the blood of Jesus Christ” (v. 2). |
| Who is identified as begetting believers again? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | God, according to abundant mercy (v. 3). |
| Is the resurrection of Jesus Christ central to living hope? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “By the resurrection of Jesus Christ from the dead” (v. 3). |
| Is the inheritance secure and future-oriented? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | It is described as reserved in heaven (v. 4). |
| Who guards the believers? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | They are guarded by God's power (v. 5). |
| Is faith included in that guarding? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | They are guarded “through faith” (v. 5). |
| Does the passage identify faith as the independent originating cause of election or new birth? | NOT ESTABLISHED | The passage attributes election to relation with the Father's foreknowledge and new birth to God's abundant mercy. |
| Does the passage erase human faith or obedience? | EXPLICITLY DENIED | Obedience (v. 2) and faith (v. 5) are expressly included. |
| Does the passage settle the complete chronology of regeneration and faith? | UNRESOLVED | The grammatical forms do not provide a complete temporal mechanism. |
| Does the passage exhaustively define foreknowledge? | UNRESOLVED | The noun and genitive construction do not settle all theological interpretations. |
| Does the passage teach that believers experience no suffering? | EXPLICITLY DENIED BY CONTEXT | Verses 6–7 acknowledge grief and the testing of faith. |

### I. GUA-W recognition and Triple Constraint disposition

The passage preserves a clear distinction between originating divine action and derivative human response:

- The Father is identified in relation to foreknowledge.
- The Spirit is identified in relation to sanctification.
- Jesus Christ is identified in relation to His blood and resurrection.
- God, according to abundant mercy, begets believers again.
- God's power guards the believers through faith.
- Obedience and faith remain real elements of the described saving life.

This is consistent with the governing distinction **Author / Source ≠ witness or response**. It is a textual recognition within the existing GUA-W vocabulary, not a proposal for a new layer, operator, Gear, or architectural amendment.

**Biblical compliance — PASS, within scope.** The reading preserves election, the Father's foreknowledge, the Spirit's sanctification, Christ's blood and resurrection, God's begetting action, human obedience and faith, and the future revelation of salvation. It does not make faith the stated ground of election, and it does not erase faith from the passage.

**Scientific coherence — PASS, within scope.** The grammatical observations distinguish active and passive forms, prepositional relations, participial aspect, and interpretive limits. No empirical scientific claim is derived from the theological text, and no analogy is presented as scientific proof.

**GUA-W consistency — PASS, within scope.** God is the source of mercy, new birth, and preserving power; Christ's blood and resurrection are central; the Spirit's sanctifying work is named; faith and obedience are real responses within the saving order. No architectural change is proposed.

### J. Entry finding and limits

**Finding:** 1 Peter 1:1–5 presents believers as elect according to the foreknowledge of God the Father, in relation to the sanctification of the Spirit and unto obedience and the sprinkling of Jesus Christ's blood. Peter then praises God for begetting believers again according to abundant mercy, unto living hope through the resurrection of Jesus Christ. Their inheritance is reserved, and they are guarded by God's power through faith toward the salvation to be revealed in the last time.

The passage does not identify independent human will or foreseen autonomous faith as the originating cause of election or new birth. It does explicitly include faith and obedience. It therefore must not be used either to make human faith the source of God's mercy or to erase the human believing and obedience that Peter names.

**Status:** PASS for election being related to the Father's foreknowledge; PASS for the Spirit's sanctifying work and Christ's blood; PASS for God's active begetting according to abundant mercy; PASS for Christ's resurrection as the basis of living hope; PASS for God's power guarding believers through faith; UNRESOLVED for the complete semantic scope of foreknowledge and the precise chronology of sanctification, new birth, and first faith; NOT ESTABLISHED for the claim that God elected on the basis of foreseen autonomous faith.

**No GUA-W architectural change is proposed.** HW-018 is one investigation of 1 Peter 1:1–5. Future discoveries concerning these same verses must be added to this entry, not opened as another investigation.



### Canonical completion review — 2026-11-18

**Original limitation reviewed:** ** The semantic scope of foreknowledge and the relation of sanctification, new birth, faith, and preservation need the wider Petrine and canonical context.

**Canonical passages examined:** **1 Peter 1:6–12, 18–25; 2:1–10; Romans 8:28–30 (HW-016); Ephesians 1:3–14 (HW-017); 2 Thessalonians 2:13–14 (HW-015); John 1:12–13 (HW-001); John 3:3–8 (HW-005); Titus 3:3–7 (HW-019); Romans 10:13–17.**

**Combined biblical finding:** First Peter's immediate continuation includes tested faith, love for Christ, salvation, the prophets' testimony, and new birth through the Word. Romans 8 and Ephesians 1 provide related purpose/election accounts. John 1 and 3 clarify divine source and Spirit-birth. Titus names regeneration and renewal by the Spirit. Romans 10 supplies the proclamation/hearing/believing relationship.

**Revised disposition:** **NARROWED.** The Father's foreknowledge, the Spirit's sanctification, Christ's blood/resurrection, God's begetting, and preservation through faith are explicit. The passage does not state that election is based on foreseen autonomous faith. The full semantic scope of foreknowledge and exact chronology remain not exhaustively defined.

**Synchronization instruction:** This disposition supplements the original passage analysis and governs the classification of the limitation above. Where an earlier table says only “not established by this passage alone” or “unresolved,” read that statement as a limit of the assigned passage, not as a conclusion about the whole canon. The cited passages must remain in their own contexts. Any remaining question is limited to the precise boundary stated here; no duplicate HW investigation is created.

## 25. Entry — HW-019: Titus 3:3–7 (2026-11-18)

### A. Exact KJV passage

> **Titus 3:3–7**
>
> 3 For we ourselves also were sometimes foolish, disobedient, deceived, serving divers lusts and pleasures, living in malice and envy, hateful, and hating one another.
>
> 4 But after that the kindness and love of God our Saviour toward man appeared,
>
> 5 Not by works of righteousness which we have done, but according to his mercy he saved us, by the washing of regeneration, and renewing of the Holy Ghost;
>
> 6 Which he shed on us abundantly through Jesus Christ our Saviour;
>
> 7 That being justified by his grace, we should be made heirs according to the hope of eternal life.

### B. Passage boundary and immediate context

The assigned examination is Titus 3:3–7. The immediate context is Titus 3:1–8.

Verses 1–2 instruct believers to be subject to principalities and powers, obey magistrates, be ready to every good work, speak evil of no man, avoid being contentious, be gentle, and show meekness toward all men. Verse 8 returns to the practical consequence: those who have believed God should be careful to maintain good works. The passage therefore does not set salvation and conduct against one another. It distinguishes the source of salvation from the good works expected in the life of those who have believed.

Verse 3 explains the former condition with a shared “we ourselves also.” Paul includes himself and his fellow believers in the description; the former condition is not assigned only to an outside class. Verses 4–7 then establish the contrast: God's kindness and love appeared; He saved according to mercy, not according to righteous works done by us; the saving action is described in relation to regeneration and renewal of the Holy Ghost, the Spirit's abundant outpouring through Jesus Christ, justification by grace, and inheritance according to the hope of eternal life.

### C. Original-language observations

**Verse 3 — the former condition**

- **ἦμεν (*ēmen*)**, “we were”: imperfect active indicative, first person plural, from εἰμί (*eimi*), “to be.” Paul describes the former state as continuing in the past.
- **ἀνόητοι (*anoētoi*)**, “foolish”: nominative masculine plural adjective. It describes a failure of understanding or discernment; it is not a technical definition of every human cognitive capacity.
- **ἀπειθεῖς (*apeitheis*)**, “disobedient”: nominative masculine plural adjective, conveying disobedience or unwillingness to be persuaded. It identifies a moral and relational condition.
- **πλανώμενοι (*planōmenoi*)**, “deceived”: present middle/passive participle, nominative masculine plural, from πλανάω (*planaō*). The form describes those being led astray or deceived; the exact force is passive in this context.
- **δουλεύοντες (*douleuontes*)**, “serving”: present active participle, nominative masculine plural, from δουλεύω (*douleuō*), “to serve / be enslaved.” The former life is described as active service to diverse lusts and pleasures.
- **ἐπιθυμίαις (*epithymiais*)** and **ἡδοναῖς (*hēdonais*)**, “lusts” and “pleasures”: dative plural nouns. They identify the desires and pleasures to which the former life was enslaved.
- **διάγοντες (*diagontes*)**, “living”: present active participle, nominative masculine plural, from διάγω (*diagō*). The text describes a lived pattern characterized by malice and envy.
- **μισοῦντες (*misountes*)**, “hating”: present active participle, nominative masculine plural, from μισέω (*miseō*). Paul describes reciprocal hostility: hateful and hating one another.

The former condition includes deception and disobedience, but also active serving, living, and hating. The text does not portray people as without desire or action. Nor does it say that these actions are the source of salvation. The former conduct is a description of the condition from which God saves.

**Verse 4 — divine appearance and initiative**

- **ὅτε δὲ (*hote de*)**, “But after that / but when”: introduces the contrast and transition from the former condition to God's saving action.
- **ἡ χρηστότης (*hē chrēstotēs*)**, “kindness”: nominative feminine singular noun. It identifies God's benevolent kindness.
- **ἡ φιλανθρωπία (*hē philanthrōpia*)**, “love toward man / philanthropy”: nominative feminine singular noun. The term expresses God's benevolent love toward humanity.
- **ἐπεφάνη (*epephanē*)**, “appeared”: aorist passive-form verb, third person singular, from ἐπιφαίνω (*epiphainō*). It describes the manifestation or appearance of God's kindness and love. The passive form does not make the kindness its own independent agent; God is the one whose kindness and love are in view.
- The KJV identifies God as “our Saviour” in verse 4. Verse 6 also names Jesus Christ as “our Saviour.” The text should preserve both designations and the explicit mediation through Jesus Christ in verse 6.

**Verse 5 — source, means, and saving action**

- **οὐκ ἐξ ἔργων (*ouk ex ergōn*)**, “not by / not out of works”: ἐκ (*ek*) with the genitive marks source or origin. Paul explicitly denies that salvation originates from works of righteousness which we ourselves have done.
- **τῶν ἐν δικαιοσύνῃ ἃ ἐποιήσαμεν ἡμεῖς**, “of righteousness which we have done”: the aorist active indicative **ἐποιήσαμεν (*epoiēsamen*)**, first person plural, identifies the works as actions we performed. Paul excludes those works as the source or basis of the saving action.
- **ἀλλὰ κατὰ τὸ αὐτοῦ ἔλεος (*alla kata to autou eleos*)**, “but according to his mercy”: κατά with the accusative expresses accordance with or basis according to which the saving action occurs. The explicit alternative to our righteous works is God's mercy.
- **ἔσωσεν (*esōsen*)**, “he saved”: aorist active indicative, third person singular, from σῴζω (*sōzō*). God is the subject carried forward from verse 4; “us” is the direct object. The sentence attributes the saving action to God.
- **διὰ λουτροῦ παλιγγενεσίας (*dia loutrou palingenesias*)**, “by/through the washing of regeneration”: διά with the genitive marks means or mediation. **λουτρόν (*loutron*)** means washing or bath; **παλιγγενεσία (*palingenesia*)** means regeneration, rebirth, or renewal of life. The phrase identifies regeneration as integral to the saving action. The wording alone does not settle every debate about the relation of this washing to water baptism, cleansing imagery, or the precise order of conversion.
- **καὶ ἀνακαινώσεως πνεύματος ἁγίου (*kai anakainōseōs pneumatos hagiou*)**, “and renewing of the Holy Ghost”: **ἀνακαίνωσις (*anakainōsis*)** means renewal or making new. The genitive “of the Holy Ghost” places this renewal in relation to the Holy Spirit. The paired terms “washing of regeneration” and “renewing of the Holy Ghost” belong together in Paul's description; the syntax should not be forced to prove that they are two entirely separate chronological events.

**Verse 6 — the Spirit poured out through Christ**

- **οὗ / ὃ** in the relative construction connects the abundant outpouring to the preceding reference to the Holy Ghost; the KJV renders the clause “Which he shed on us abundantly.” The subject of the outpouring is God, carried forward from the saving action.
- **ἐξέχεεν (*execheen*)**, “he shed / poured out”: aorist active indicative, third person singular, from ἐκχέω (*ekcheō*). The verb depicts abundant outpouring.
- **πλουσίως (*plousiōs*)**, “abundantly”: adverb modifying the outpouring.
- **διὰ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ (*dia Iēsou Christou*)**, “through Jesus Christ”: διά with the genitive identifies Jesus Christ as the mediator through whom the Spirit is poured out. The verse expressly connects God's saving work and the Spirit's outpouring with Jesus Christ our Saviour.

**Verse 7 — justification and inheritance**

- **δικαιωθέντες (*dikaiōthentes*)**, “being justified”: aorist passive participle, nominative masculine plural, from δικαιόω (*dikaioō*). The passive form describes the recipients as those justified; they are not presented as the originating justifier.
- **τῇ ἐκείνου χάριτι (*tē ekeinou chariti*)**, “by his grace”: the dative identifies grace as the basis or means in the description of justification. The antecedent is God our Saviour.
- **κληρονόμοι (*klēronomoi*)**, “heirs”: nominative masculine plural, describing the status given to those justified.
- **γενηθῶμεν (*genēthōmen*)**, “we should be made / become”: aorist passive subjunctive, first person plural, from γίνομαι (*ginomai*), in the purpose clause introduced by ἵνα. The clause expresses the intended result that those justified by grace should become heirs.
- **κατ᾽ ἐλπίδα ζωῆς αἰωνίου (*kat’ elpida zōēs aiōniou*)**, “according to the hope of eternal life”: the inheritance is oriented toward the hope of eternal life.

### D. What the passage establishes

1. **The former condition:** foolishness, disobedience, deception, enslavement to desires and pleasures, malice, envy, hatred, and reciprocal hostility (v. 3).
2. **The turning point:** God's kindness and love toward humanity appeared (v. 4).
3. **The source of salvation:** God's mercy, not righteous works performed by us (v. 5).
4. **The saving action:** God saved us (v. 5).
5. **Regeneration and renewal:** the saving action is described through the washing of regeneration and renewal of the Holy Ghost (v. 5).
6. **The Spirit's outpouring:** the Holy Ghost is poured out abundantly through Jesus Christ our Saviour (v. 6).
7. **Justification:** recipients are justified by God's grace (v. 7).
8. **Inheritance:** those justified by grace are made heirs according to the hope of eternal life (v. 7).
9. **Good works:** the immediate context commands readiness for good works (v. 1), and verse 8 says believers should maintain good works. Those works are the fitting consequence of God's saving mercy, not the originating source of salvation.

### E. Human will, desire, and response

Titus 3:3–7 does not offer a formal definition of the human will. It does, however, describe several aspects of human willing and conduct:

- The former condition includes foolishness and disobedience.
- People are described as deceived.
- People are actively serving desires and pleasures.
- People are living in malice and envy and hating one another.
- Paul includes himself and his fellow believers in the former condition: “we ourselves also.”
- The text does not say that those former desires or actions produced salvation.
- The decisive saving action is attributed to God, according to His mercy.
- The passage does not name faith as a term in verses 3–7; faith is named in verse 8 (“they which have believed God”) in the immediate continuation.
- Good works are commanded and maintained by believers, but are not the source of the salvation described in verse 5.

The passage therefore does not erase human desire, conduct, or response. It denies that human righteous works are the originating source of salvation and identifies God's mercy as the basis of His saving action.

### F. Regeneration, renewal, and the order of salvation

The phrase “washing of regeneration, and renewing of the Holy Ghost” is one of the most direct statements in this study concerning regeneration and the Spirit's renewing work.

What the passage explicitly states:
- God saved us according to His mercy.
- The saving action is described through the washing of regeneration and renewal of the Holy Ghost.
- God poured out the Holy Ghost abundantly through Jesus Christ.
- Those justified by grace become heirs according to the hope of eternal life.

What the passage does not explicitly specify:
- A complete measurable chronology between regeneration, renewal, faith, justification, and every other aspect of conversion.
- Whether “washing” should be identified exclusively with water baptism, inward cleansing, or a combined baptismal/cleansing image.
- Whether regeneration and renewal are two distinct successive events or closely joined descriptions of the same divine saving work.
- The precise relation of the Spirit's renewal to the first act of faith.

The aorist forms present the saving action and outpouring as events viewed as a whole; they do not, by themselves, provide a full theological timeline. The text must be allowed to state God's action without forcing a chronological scheme into the grammar.

### G. Canonical comparisons — comparison only, not duplicate investigations

- **John 1:12–13 (HW-001):** Receiving and believing are real responses, while birth is explicitly said to be not of the will of the flesh or of man, but of God. This comparison must preserve both statements.
- **John 3:1–8 (HW-005):** Jesus teaches the necessity of birth from above/from the Spirit. Titus 3 adds the language of regeneration and renewal of the Holy Ghost. The two entries complement one another without becoming duplicate examinations.
- **John 6:35–45, 63–65 (HW-006):** Jesus speaks of the Father's giving and drawing and the Spirit's quickening. This remains a cross-reference to the completed entry.
- **Romans 9:6–24 (HW-007):** Paul denies that mercy originates in the one who wills or runs and asserts God's authority to show mercy. Titus 3 states salvation according to God's mercy rather than our righteous works. The two passages have distinct arguments and must not be collapsed.
- **Ephesians 2:1–10 (HW-008):** God makes alive those dead in trespasses and sins; salvation is by grace through faith and not of works. Titus 3 likewise identifies mercy rather than our righteous works as the source of salvation and names regeneration and renewal.
- **James 1:16–18 (HW-009):** James says God begat us of His own will with the word of truth. Titus 3 describes salvation according to God's mercy through regeneration and renewal of the Holy Ghost.
- **Philippians 2:12–13 (HW-010):** God works in believers both to will and to do of His good pleasure. Titus 3 does not explicitly state that same willing/doctrine, but its account of mercy, regeneration, and renewal is consistent with examining the source of renewed life before considering the believer's subsequent conduct.
- **2 Thessalonians 2:13–14 (HW-015):** God's choice unto salvation is described with sanctification of the Spirit and belief of the truth, followed by calling through the Gospel. Titus 3 names regeneration and renewal by the Holy Ghost; neither text should be made to state a chronology it does not expressly give.
- **Romans 8:28–30 (HW-016), Ephesians 1:3–14 (HW-017), and 1 Peter 1:1–5 (HW-018):** These entries establish further statements concerning God's purpose, election, foreknowledge, calling, new birth, and preservation. They remain completed investigations and are cross-referenced only.

### G1. Cross-reference completion audit — regeneration, faith, and justification

The statement that Titus 3:3–7 does not provide a complete chronology is correct as a statement about this unit alone. It is not the end of the canonical investigation. The following passages supply additional information and must be read in their own contexts.

| Missing information in Titus 3:3–7 | Relevant passage(s) | What the passage(s) establish | Combined finding | What remains unresolved |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| What does Jesus say about the necessity and source of new birth? | **John 3:3–8 (HW-005)** | Jesus says a man must be born again/from above and identifies birth of the Spirit as necessary. The Spirit's work is not described as a product of flesh. | Titus's “washing of regeneration, and renewing of the Holy Ghost” belongs with Jesus' teaching that entrance into the kingdom requires birth from the Spirit. | John 3 does not give a complete temporal sequence relating the first act of faith to regeneration. |
| How are receiving/believing and birth related? | **John 1:12–13 (HW-001)** | Those who receive Christ and believe on His name are described as born not of blood, nor of the will of the flesh, nor of the will of man, but of God. | The text explicitly affirms receiving and believing while attributing the source of the birth to God. Human response and divine source must both remain in the account. | The two verses do not state a measurable interval or exhaustive chronological mechanism between birth and believing. |
| What is said about being made alive and faith in salvation? | **Ephesians 2:1–10 (HW-008)** | Verses 1–5 describe people dead in trespasses and sins and God making them alive with Christ; verses 8–9 say salvation is by grace through faith, not of works. | This supplies the divine life-giving action and the stated relation of faith to salvation. Faith is not a work that gives humans grounds for boasting. | The passage does not spell out every temporal detail of regeneration and the first act of faith. |
| What is said directly about faith and justification? | **Romans 4:4–5; Romans 5:1** | Romans 4 contrasts wages owed for work with faith counted for righteousness to the one who believes on Him that justifies the ungodly. Romans 5:1 states, “being justified by faith, we have peace with God through our Lord Jesus Christ.” | These passages directly identify faith's relation to justification. They clarify a relation that Titus 3:7 names as justification by grace but does not describe in terms of faith. | They do not, by themselves, give a complete chronology of regeneration in relation to the first act of faith. |
| What does the New Testament say about God's begetting and the Word? | **James 1:18 (HW-009); 1 Peter 1:23 (compare HW-018's context, but 1 Peter 1:23 is outside its assigned unit)** | James says God begat us with the word of truth. First Peter 1:23 describes being born again by the incorruptible seed, by the word of God. | These passages add the Word's role in the account of divine begetting/new birth. They should be considered alongside Titus's regeneration and renewal by the Holy Ghost. | Neither statement alone supplies a complete chronology of new birth and the first act of faith. |
| How does the Spirit's work relate to belief? | **2 Thessalonians 2:13–14 (HW-015)** | Paul joins God's choice unto salvation with sanctification of the Spirit and belief of the truth, then speaks of God's calling through the Gospel. | The Spirit's sanctifying work, belief of the truth, and Gospel calling are all included in Paul's account. This helps prevent separating divine action from the believing response. | The passage does not define every temporal or causal detail between sanctification, regeneration, and faith. |

**Combined finding:** Scripture supplies more information than Titus 3:3–7 supplies by itself. The wider testimony identifies the necessity and divine source of new birth (John 3:3–8; John 1:12–13), God's life-giving action and salvation through faith (Ephesians 2:1–10), faith's relation to justification (Romans 4:4–5; 5:1), God's begetting through the Word (James 1:18; 1 Peter 1:23), and the relation of the Spirit's sanctifying work, belief, and Gospel calling (2 Thessalonians 2:13–14).

Therefore, the broad relationships are not left wholly unresolved. The more precise temporal order and complete causal mechanics between regeneration and the first act of faith are **not fully specified by these passages as a combined set**. We must state the relations Scripture does establish and reserve only the details it does not disclose.

This audit does not create new HW entries or reopen completed investigations. It cross-references existing entries and adds unnumbered references where appropriate.

### H. What the passage does not establish

| Question | Finding | Textual limit |
|---|---|---|
| Are human beings described as active in their former desires and conduct? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | Serving, living, and hating are active descriptions (v. 3). |
| Does Paul include himself among those formerly in this condition? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “We ourselves also” (v. 3). |
| Are righteous works the source of salvation? | EXPLICITLY DENIED | “Not by works of righteousness which we have done” (v. 5). |
| Is God's mercy the stated basis of salvation? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “According to his mercy he saved us” (v. 5). |
| Is regeneration named? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “Washing of regeneration” (v. 5). |
| Is the Holy Ghost's renewing work named? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | “Renewing of the Holy Ghost” (v. 5). |
| Is the Spirit poured out through Jesus Christ? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | Verse 6. |
| Is justification by grace named? | EXPLICITLY ESTABLISHED | Verse 7. |
| Are faith and believing named in the assigned verses 3–7? | NOT IN THIS UNIT | Believing appears in the immediate context, verse 8. |
| Does the passage settle the complete chronology of regeneration and faith? | UNRESOLVED | No full temporal sequence is stated. |
| Does “washing” alone settle the baptism question? | UNRESOLVED | The phrase's imagery and theological application require wider canonical examination. |
| Does the passage define every component of human will? | UNRESOLVED | It describes former desire and conduct but is not a comprehensive anthropology. |
| Are good works irrelevant after salvation? | EXPLICITLY DENIED BY CONTEXT | Verses 1 and 8 call believers to good works; verse 5 excludes them as salvation's source. |

### I. GUA-W recognition and Triple Constraint disposition

The text establishes a distinction between the source of salvation and the conduct of those saved:

- The former condition is characterized by disordered desire, deception, and disobedience.
- God's kindness and love appear.
- God saves according to His mercy, not according to our righteous works.
- Regeneration and renewal of the Holy Ghost are named within the saving action.
- The Spirit is poured out abundantly through Jesus Christ.
- Justification is by grace.
- Those justified become heirs of eternal life.
- Believers are instructed to maintain good works.

**Biblical compliance — PASS, within scope.** The reading preserves both the active human conduct in verse 3 and the explicit divine agency in verses 4–7. It does not turn good works into the source of salvation or erase the good works expected of believers.

**Scientific coherence — PASS, within scope.** The analysis distinguishes grammatical subject, object, active and passive forms, prepositional relations, and the limits of aspect. It makes no empirical scientific claim from the theological text.

**GUA-W consistency — PASS, within scope.** God is the stated source of mercy and salvation. Regeneration and renewal are attributed to the Holy Ghost, and the Spirit's outpouring is through Jesus Christ. Human conduct and good works remain real, but are not the originating cause of salvation.

No new GUA-W layer, operator, Gear, or architectural change is proposed.

### J. Entry finding and limits

**Finding:** Titus 3:3–7 describes humanity's former condition as foolish, disobedient, deceived, and enslaved to diverse desires and pleasures, with malice, envy, and mutual hatred. Paul includes himself and his fellow believers in this description. The turning point is God's kindness and love appearing. God saves us according to His mercy, not according to righteous works that we have done. This saving action is described through the washing of regeneration and renewal of the Holy Ghost, whom God poured out abundantly through Jesus Christ our Saviour. Those justified by God's grace become heirs according to the hope of eternal life.

The passage does not identify human righteous works as the source of salvation. It does not erase human desire, conduct, or the believer's subsequent responsibility to good works. It names regeneration and renewal by the Holy Ghost, but does not settle the complete chronology of regeneration, faith, and justification, nor does it by itself settle the full meaning of the washing imagery.

**Status:** PASS for God's mercy as the stated basis of salvation; PASS for the explicit exclusion of human righteous works as its source; PASS for regeneration and renewal of the Holy Ghost; PASS for the Spirit's abundant outpouring through Jesus Christ; PASS for justification by grace and inheritance of eternal life; PASS for preserving human conduct and subsequent good works; UNRESOLVED for the complete chronology of regeneration, faith, and justification, the precise relation between washing and baptism, and an exhaustive definition of human will.

**No GUA-W architectural change is proposed.** HW-019 is one investigation of Titus 3:3–7. Any future discoveries concerning these same verses must be added to this entry, not opened as another investigation.



### Canonical completion review — 2026-11-18

**Original limitation reviewed:** ** The relation of regeneration, faith, justification, and the washing imagery requires the wider canon.

**Canonical passages examined:** **John 1:12–13 (HW-001); John 3:3–8 (HW-005); Ephesians 2:1–10 (HW-008); Romans 4:4–5; 5:1; James 1:18 (HW-009); 1 Peter 1:23; 2 Thessalonians 2:13–14 (HW-015); 1 John 5:1 (HW-014); Romans 6:1–4; Acts 2:38–41; Ephesians 5:25–27; Hebrews 10:19–22.**

**Combined biblical finding:** The first set supplies the source of new birth, the Spirit's work, divine life-giving, faith's relation to justification, and begetting through the Word. Romans 6, Acts 2, Ephesians 5, and Hebrews 10 add baptismal, cleansing, and washing imagery in their own contexts. They must be compared before deciding whether Titus's “washing” refers exclusively to water baptism, inward cleansing, or a joined sacramental/cleansing image.

**Revised disposition:** **NARROWED.** The broad relations of mercy, regeneration, Spirit-renewal, faith, justification by grace, and good works are clarified. The exact chronology and full causal mechanics are not exhaustively specified. The baptism/washing question remains an interpretive question requiring careful comparison of the cited texts; no single cross-reference should be made to decide it by itself.

**Synchronization instruction:** This disposition supplements the original passage analysis and governs the classification of the limitation above. Where an earlier table says only “not established by this passage alone” or “unresolved,” read that statement as a limit of the assigned passage, not as a conclusion about the whole canon. The cited passages must remain in their own contexts. Any remaining question is limited to the precise boundary stated here; no duplicate HW investigation is created.

## 26. Entry — HW-020: Philippians 1:29 (2026-11-18)

### A. Exact KJV passage

> **Philippians 1:29**
>
> 29 For unto you it is given in the behalf of Christ, not only to believe on him, but also to suffer for his sake;

### B. Claim under examination

This verse is cited to support the claim that believing in Christ is graciously granted rather than originating in an autonomous human will. Paul does not merely describe an opportunity to believe: he places “to believe on him” within what has been graciously granted. The conclusion must still preserve the reality of the recipients' own believing and must not make the verse answer questions about regeneration or chronology that it does not address.

### C. Immediate context — Philippians 1:27–30

Verse 27 calls the Philippians to conduct worthy of the Gospel, standing fast in one spirit and striving together for the faith of the Gospel. Verse 28 tells them not to be terrified by adversaries and describes the matter as a sign concerning perdition and salvation, “and that of God.” Verse 29 begins with “For” and explains the exhortation: it has been graciously granted to them not only to believe in Christ but also to suffer for His sake. Verse 30 connects their present conflict with the same struggle they had seen in Paul.

The subject is the congregation's steadfast Gospel conduct amid opposition, not an abstract philosophical definition of free will. Believing and suffering are both placed within the gracious grant. The surrounding reference to what is “of God” in verse 28 supports identifying God as the giver. However, the grammatical subject of “was granted” in verse 29 is not an expressed personal name; the immediate context supplies the identification of the source.

### D. Original Greek observations

**Greek text:** ὅτι ὑμῖν ἐχαρίσθη τὸ ὑπὲρ Χριστοῦ, οὐ μόνον τὸ εἰς αὐτὸν πιστεύειν ἀλλὰ καὶ τὸ ὑπὲρ αὐτοῦ πάσχειν

- **ὅτι (*hoti*)**, “for / because”: conjunction linking the grant to the preceding exhortation.
- **ὑμῖν (*hymin*)**, “to you”: dative plural personal pronoun. Paul addresses the Philippian believers collectively.
- **ἐχαρίσθη (*echaristhē*)**, “it was graciously granted”: aorist passive indicative, third-person singular, from **χαρίζομαι (*charizomai*)**, to grant or bestow graciously. The passive presents the recipients as receiving a grant; the verb itself does not name the giver. Verse 28's “and that of God” supplies the immediate contextual connection to God.
- **τὸ ὑπὲρ Χριστοῦ (*to hyper Christou*)**, “the matter concerning/on behalf of Christ”: introduces the content of what has been granted.
- **τὸ εἰς αὐτὸν πιστεύειν (*to eis auton pisteuein*)**, “the believing in him”: the article **τὸ** makes the infinitive phrase function substantivally. **πιστεύειν (*pisteuein*)** is a present active infinitive of **πιστεύω (*pisteuō*)**, “to believe / trust.” The action is grammatically active: the recipients genuinely believe. Its inclusion under **ἐχαρίσθη** means this believing is part of what has been graciously granted.
- **ἀλλὰ καὶ (*alla kai*)**, “but also”: introduces the second, coordinated item.
- **τὸ ὑπὲρ αὐτοῦ πάσχειν (*to hyper autou paschein*)**, “the suffering for his sake”: another articular infinitive phrase. **πάσχειν (*paschein*)** is also a present active infinitive. Suffering for Christ is included alongside believing in Christ under the same grant.

**Grammatical safeguards:**

1. The verse describes the act of believing in Christ as granted. It does not use a separate noun meaning “a quantity of faith,” nor does it define an independently bestowed faculty or measure of faith. “Believing itself is included in the grant” is the precise observation.
2. The active voice of **πιστεύειν** identifies believing as the action performed by the believers. It does not identify them as the originating source of the grant.
3. The aorist **ἐχαρίσθη** presents the grant as a whole; the aorist by itself does not establish a detailed temporal sequence between regeneration and the first act of faith.
4. The present infinitive **πιστεύειν** does not by itself establish a chronology or prove that faith is continuously or progressively imparted.
5. The passive construction does not grammatically name God as its agent. God is identified contextually, especially by verse 28, not by pretending that the passive verb contains an expressed subject “God.”

The Greek text and morphology have been cross-checked against the SBL Greek New Testament / Nestle Greek text and interlinear morphological presentations. The conclusion rests on the syntax of the sentence, not on a Strong's gloss alone.

### E. What exactly is given, and to whom?

| Question | Textual finding | Boundary |
|---|---|---|
| What is given? | The gracious grant includes both believing in Christ and suffering for His sake. | The verse describes these actions as granted; it does not define faith as an abstract substance or quantify it. |
| To whom is it given? | “Unto you” — the Philippian believers addressed by Paul, plural. | The immediate grammatical recipients are this congregation; universal application requires wider canonical examination. |
| Who gives it? | The passive verb leaves the giver unnamed in verse 29; verse 28 says the related matter is “of God,” supporting God as giver in context. | Do not claim the passive morphology alone names the agent. |
| How is believing performed? | The present active infinitive **πιστεύειν** describes the recipients' real act of believing in Christ. | Active human response does not establish independent human origination. |
| Through what means is the grant given? | No precise mechanism is specified in verse 29. The immediate context names the Gospel and its shared confession, but does not explain the complete operation by which the grant produces believing. | Do not insert a complete regeneration/faith chronology into this verse. |
| Why is suffering included? | Suffering for Christ is placed alongside believing as part of the same gracious grant, in the context of opposition and Gospel steadfastness. | This does not teach that suffering earns salvation or is identical to faith. |

### F. Canonical cross-reference completion

The question “through what means?” must not be left at “the verse does not say” without checking other passages already examined in this reference work. These passages contribute distinct pieces and must not be collapsed into one technical sequence.

| Passage | What it contributes | Boundary that must be preserved |
|---|---|---|
| **John 6:37–45, 63–65 (HW-006)** | Jesus says that coming to Him depends upon the Father's drawing and that coming is given by the Father. Verse 45 also speaks of hearing and learning from the Father. | The passage establishes divine initiative and the necessity of coming; it does not supply a complete timetable for regeneration and first faith. |
| **Acts 16:14** | The Lord opens Lydia's heart so that she attends to the things spoken by Paul. | Divine opening and attention to the preached message are joined in the narrative. The verse does not define every step between opening, regeneration, and faith. |
| **Romans 10:14–17** | Paul connects calling, believing, hearing, and preaching, and says faith comes by hearing the word of God. | The Gospel message and its proclamation are identified as the public means associated with faith. This does not make the preacher or hearer the independent originating cause of salvation. |
| **1 Corinthians 3:5–7** | Paul describes himself and Apollos as ministers “by whom ye believed,” while also saying the Lord gave to every man and that God gives the increase. | The text distinguishes human ministry as an instrument from God's action; its immediate subject is the ministers' role and the growth of God's field, not a complete account of regeneration. |
| **Ephesians 2:8–10 (HW-008)** | Salvation is by grace through faith, not of works, and the saved are God's workmanship, created in Christ Jesus unto good works. | The antecedent and scope of “that” in verse 8 require grammatical care. Do not use the verse as if its grammar alone unambiguously defines faith as the individual gift without addressing the interpretive question recorded in HW-008. |
| **2 Thessalonians 2:13–14 (HW-015)** | God's choice, sanctification of the Spirit, belief of the truth, and calling through the Gospel appear together. | The passage joins divine action, believing, and Gospel calling, but does not convert them into an exhaustive chronological chart. |

**Combined finding:** The wider testimony identifies the Gospel/Word and its proclamation as the public means through which people hear and believe, while also identifying the Father's drawing and giving, the Lord's opening of the heart, the Spirit's sanctifying work, and God's gracious action. Philippians 1:29 contributes a direct statement that believing in Christ itself is included in what has been graciously granted. Scripture therefore preserves both divine initiative and the real human act of hearing, attending, coming, repenting, and believing.

**What remains open:** These passages do not all describe the same operation or use “granting,” “drawing,” “opening,” “sanctifying,” “calling,” “hearing,” and “believing” as interchangeable terms. They establish the Gospel as a means and divine initiative as necessary, but do not provide one exhaustive technical chronology specifying every moment and causal relation between regeneration and the first act of faith. Philippians 1:29 itself does not state the precise mechanism by which believing is granted.

### G. What the passage establishes

| Category | Finding | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| Choice / response | ESTABLISHED | The Philippians actively believe in Christ and stand in the Gospel conflict. |
| Responsibility | ESTABLISHED IN CONTEXT | They are exhorted to conduct themselves worthily, stand fast, and strive together (vv. 27–28). |
| Human ability | NOT DEFINED AS AUTONOMOUS SPIRITUAL ABILITY | The verse describes believing but does not identify independent human capacity as its source. |
| Willingness | NOT EXPLICITLY ANALYZED | The passage does not give a theory of the will or explain the internal mechanics of desire. |
| Divine initiative / grant | ESTABLISHED | Believing and suffering are included in what has been graciously granted; verse 28 connects the matter with God. |
| Faith | ESTABLISHED AS A GRANTED ACT | The text explicitly includes “to believe on him” within the grant. |
| Human action | ESTABLISHED | **πιστεύειν** is active; the recipients truly believe. |
| Means of faith | NARROWED BY CANONICAL COMPARISON | Romans 10 identifies hearing the Word; Acts 16:14 identifies the Lord's opening of Lydia's heart as she attends to Paul's message. Philippians 1:29 alone does not specify the mechanism. |
| Regeneration / spiritual birth | NOT EXPLICITLY NAMED | The verse does not use birth/regeneration terminology. |
| Chronology of regeneration and faith | NOT SETTLED | Neither the aorist passive grant nor the present active infinitive supplies a complete temporal sequence. |
| Suffering for Christ | ESTABLISHED AS ALSO GRANTED | The same governing verb includes suffering for Christ; this is not a meritorious cause of salvation. |
| Salvation as a whole | NOT EXHAUSTIVELY DEFINED HERE | The passage contributes to the source of believing and the believer's suffering, not every stage of salvation. |

### H. GUA-W Triple Constraint disposition

- **Biblical compliance — PASS, within scope.** The passage expressly includes believing in Christ within what has been graciously granted. It also preserves the active believing of the recipients and the second granted matter, suffering for Christ. The surrounding context connects the matter with God. The entry does not claim that the verse states a complete chronology or mechanism.
- **Scientific coherence — PASS, within scope.** The Greek morphology and syntax support the distinction between the passive grant and the active infinitive of believing. The aorist is not used to manufacture a temporal sequence, and the passive verb is not treated as grammatically naming an unexpressed agent.
- **GUA-W consistency — PASS, within scope.** God is the contextual source of the gracious grant; the Philippians are genuine human responders who believe. The derivative response is not thereby made the Original Cause. No GUA-W layer, operator, Gear, or architectural change is proposed.

### I. Entry finding and limits

**Finding:** Philippians 1:29 states that the Philippian believers have been graciously granted not only to believe in Christ but also to suffer for His sake. The verb **ἐχαρίσθη** is passive and does not name its giver within verse 29; the preceding verse's statement that the matter is “of God” identifies God as the contextual source. The infinitive **πιστεύειν** is active: the Philippians themselves truly believe. The passage therefore places the real human act of believing within a divine grant; it does not present believing as an independently originating human achievement.

The verse does not specify the precise means or complete internal mechanism by which believing is granted. The wider canon identifies Gospel proclamation and hearing as means associated with faith, while also identifying the Father's drawing, the Lord's opening of the heart, and the Spirit's work. Those passages clarify the wider relationship but do not provide one exhaustive chronology of regeneration and first faith.

**Status:** PASS for the finding that believing in Christ is included in what is graciously granted; PASS for the distinction between divine grant and active human believing; PASS for suffering for Christ as the second item under the same grant; NARROWED by canonical comparison concerning the Gospel/Word as means and divine initiative; UNRESOLVED for the complete chronology and precise mechanism relating regeneration and the first act of faith.

**No GUA-W architectural change is proposed.** HW-020 is the single authoritative investigation of Philippians 1:29. Future discoveries concerning this verse must be added to this entry, not opened as another investigation.

### Canonical completion review — 2026-11-18

**Original limitation reviewed:** Philippians 1:29 does not specify the means or mechanism by which believing is granted, nor does it state the complete chronology of regeneration and first faith.

**Canonical passages examined:** John 6:37–45, 63–65 (HW-006); Acts 16:14; Romans 10:14–17; 1 Corinthians 3:5–7; Ephesians 2:8–10 (HW-008); 2 Thessalonians 2:13–14 (HW-015).

**Combined biblical finding:** The Gospel is publicly preached and heard; faith is connected with hearing the Word (Romans 10:14–17). The Lord opens Lydia's heart as she attends to Paul's message (Acts 16:14). The Father draws and gives in the coming to Christ (John 6:37–45, 63–65). Paul distinguishes the human ministers through whom the Corinthians believed from God's giving of the increase (1 Corinthians 3:5–7). Second Thessalonians joins Spirit-sanctification, belief of the truth, and calling through the Gospel. These passages clarify means and divine initiative without making the human act of believing unreal.

**Revised disposition:** **NARROWED.** The canonical testimony supplies the public means of Gospel proclamation/hearing and further statements of divine initiative. The precise mechanism and exhaustive chronology of regeneration and the first act of faith remain not fully specified. No passage should be made to provide a technical sequence it does not state.

**Synchronization instruction:** This review governs the classification of the limitation above. “Not specified in Philippians 1:29” is a passage-level boundary, not a claim that Scripture says nothing about the means of faith. The combined testimony clarifies the Word/Gospel means and divine initiative, while preserving the exact remaining chronological and causal limits. No duplicate HW investigation is created.


## 27. Repository-wide Cross-Reference Completion Audit — HW-001–HW-020 (2026-11-18)

### Purpose and audit standard

This audit applies the Canonical Cross-Reference Completion Rule retrospectively to all twenty numbered Human Will investigations. The aim is not to make every question appear settled. It is to distinguish what an individual passage says from what the combined biblical testimony supplies.

Each entry below is reviewed against its own unresolved questions, existing comparisons, and the wider canonical context. References to an existing HW entry are cross-references, not new investigations. References outside an existing entry's assigned passage are supporting texts only; they do not silently enlarge that entry's passage boundary.

**Status vocabulary:** **ANSWERED BY CANONICAL COMPARISON** means the combined cited passages directly address the stated question. **NARROWED** means the additional passages clarify part of the question but do not settle every detail. **REMAINS OPEN** means the cited testimony does not disclose the precise information sought. A question may not be called unresolved merely because its answer is absent from the single passage under examination.

### Audit register

| Existing entry and original limitation | Canonical passages to read together | What the combined testimony establishes | Revised disposition |
|---|---|---|---|
| **HW-001 — John 1:12–13.** The relation between receiving/believing and birth from God is not fully explained by these two verses alone. | **John 3:3–8, 14–21 (HW-005); John 6:35–45, 63–65 (HW-006); 1 John 5:1 (HW-014); James 1:18 (HW-009); 1 Peter 1:23; Ephesians 2:1–10 (HW-008).** | John 1 itself distinguishes receiving and believing from the source of birth, which is not the will of the flesh or man but God. John 3 identifies birth from the Spirit as necessary and then speaks of believing in the Son. First John 5:1 identifies the believer as born of God. James and Peter connect divine begetting/new birth with God's Word. Ephesians describes God making the dead alive and salvation through faith. | **NARROWED.** Scripture establishes divine source, the necessity and reality of believing, and the Word/Spirit's role in new birth. The exact temporal interval and complete causal mechanics between regeneration and the first act of faith are not stated in one exhaustive formula. Do not claim the two verses alone settle that chronology. |
| **HW-002 — Deuteronomy 30:11–20.** The relation between Israel's commanded choice and God's action upon the heart requires the chapter's wider covenant setting. | **Deuteronomy 10:12–16; 30:1–10, especially 30:6; Jeremiah 31:31–34; Ezekiel 36:24–28; Romans 10:6–13.** | Deuteronomy 30:6 expressly promises that the LORD will circumcise Israel's heart so that Israel will love Him; the command to choose life in 30:19 must be read alongside that promise. Jeremiah and Ezekiel describe covenant renewal and God's action in giving a new heart and Spirit. Romans 10 applies Deuteronomy's word-near-you language to the word of faith concerning Christ, while preserving Paul's own Gospel context. | **ANSWERED as to whether the covenant Scriptures also attribute heart-renewal to God: YES. NARROWED as to the relation of commanded choice and divine heart-work.** The original passage remains a real covenant summons with real responsibility. It does not, by itself, define the entire order of Christian regeneration. |
| **HW-003 — Joshua 24:14–28.** The meaning and scope of “choose” and “ye cannot serve the LORD” must be held together; the passage does not itself define universal metaphysical inability. | **Joshua 24:1–13, 19–27; Deuteronomy 30:6; Judges 2:6–23; Ezekiel 36:24–28.** | Joshua 24:1–13 grounds Israel's response in the LORD's prior acts of calling, deliverance, preservation, and gift. Verses 19–27 retain the warning about the LORD's holiness, the people's covenant unfaithfulness, and the need to put away foreign gods. Deuteronomy 30:6 and Ezekiel 36 show that Scripture also speaks of God's action upon the heart. Judges 2 records the subsequent history of covenant failure. | **NARROWED.** The chapter establishes accountable covenant choice and warns against treating a verbal pledge as proof of faithful service. It does not teach that autonomous choice originates salvation, nor does “cannot” in this covenant warning by itself define every person's metaphysical ability in every spiritual matter. |

First Timothy and Second Corinthians add apostolic testimony about Eve's deception while Romans 5 places Adam's trespass in the Adam/Christ comparison. Romans 8 describes creation's subjection and hope in the wider consequence of the Fall. | **NARROWED.** The canonical record confirms deception, human transgression, Adam's representative role in Paul's argument, and consequences beyond Genesis 3:1–7. The claim that Eve formally asserted a complete philosophical authority to define good and evil remains an inference, not an explicit sentence in Genesis 3:1–7. |
